Free Perpetuities I: Existence, Subordination and Tail Asymptotics Thanks: KSz: This research was funded in part by National Science Centre, Poland WEAVE-UNISONO grant BOOMER 2022/04/Y/ST1/00008. BK: This research was funded in part by National Science Centre, Poland, 2023/51/B/ST1/01535. For the purpose of Open Access, the authors have applied a CC-BY public copyright licence to any Author Accepted Manuscript (AAM) version arising from this submission.
Serban Belinschi, Bartosz Kołodziejek, Kamil Szpojankowski

Abstract
. We study the free analogue of the classical affine fixed-point (or perpetuity) equation 𝕏 = d 𝔸 1 / 2 ​ 𝕏 ​ 𝔸 1 / 2 + 𝔹 , {\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}\,{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{B}}, where 𝕏 {\mathbb{X}} is assumed to be ∗ * -free from the pair ( 𝔸 , 𝔹 ) ({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}}) , with 𝔸 ≥ 0 {\mathbb{A}}\geq 0 and 𝔹 = 𝔹 ∗ {\mathbb{B}}={\mathbb{B}}^{*} . Our analysis covers both the subcritical regime, where τ ⁡ ( 𝔸 ) < 1 \tau({\mathbb{A}})<1 , and the critical case τ ⁡ ( 𝔸 ) = 1 \tau({\mathbb{A}})=1 , in which the solution 𝕏 {\mathbb{X}} is necessarily unbounded. When τ ⁡ ( 𝔸 ) = 1 \tau({\mathbb{A}})=1 , we prove that the series defining 𝕏 {\mathbb{X}} converges bilaterally almost uniformly (and almost uniformly under additional tail assumptions), while the perpetuity fails to have higher moments even if all moments of 𝔸 {\mathbb{A}} and 𝔹 {\mathbb{B}} exist. Our approach relies on a detailed study of the asymptotic behavior of moments under free multiplicative convolution, which reveals a markedly different behavior from the classical setting. By employing subordination techniques for non-commutative random variables, we derive precise asymptotic estimates for the tail of the distributions of 𝕏 {\mathbb{X}} in both one-sided and symmetric cases. Interestingly, in the critical case, the free perpetuity exhibits a power-law tail behavior that mirrors the phenomenon observed in the celebrated Kesten’s theorem.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 46L54; Secondary 60E05.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Problem description and motivation

Let
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})
be a pair of self-adjoint random variables, where
{\mathbb{A}}
is positive. In this paper we study the solution of the free analogue of the classical affine fixed-point equation and investigate properties of the distribution of
{\mathbb{X}}
satisfying

(1.1)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{B}},

where
{\mathbb{X}}
is assumed to be
*
-free from the pair
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})
. When
{\mathbb{X}}
satisfies (
1.1
), we refer to it as a free perpetuity.

In the classical setting, given a pair of random variables
({\mathbf{A}},\underline{B})
, with
{\mathbf{A}}
an
N\times N
matrix,
\underline{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
a perpetuity is defined as a solution to

(1.2)

\displaystyle\underline{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbf{A}}\,\underline{X}+\underline{B},

where
\underline{X}
and the pair
({\mathbf{A}},\underline{B})
are independent.

Perpetuities have long been an active area of research in probability theory, with applications ranging from queueing theory to financial and actuarial mathematics. Once the existence of
\underline{X}
is established, one typically investigates how its distribution depends on that of
({\mathbf{A}},\underline{B})
—with particular emphasis on the asymptotic tail behavior of
\underline{X}
, see below.

In the classical setting, the usual strategy to prove the existence of
\underline{X}
is to iterate (
1.2
). Let
\{({\mathbf{A}}_{n},\underline{B}_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
be an i.i.d. sequence of copies of
({\mathbf{A}},\underline{B})
and define

\Pi_{0}=1,\quad\Pi_{n}={\mathbf{A}}_{1}\cdots{\mathbf{A}}_{n},\quad n\geq 1.

Let
|\cdot|
denote any norm on
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
and
\|\cdot\|
the corresponding operator norm.
Under the conditions

-\infty\leq{\mathbb{E}}[\log\|{\mathbf{A}}\|]<0,\quad{\mathbb{E}}[\log^{+}|\underline{B}|]<+\infty

one can show that the series

\underline{S}=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\Pi_{n-1}\underline{B}_{n},

converges almost surely, and the unique solution of (
1.2
) is given by
\underline{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\underline{S}
(see, e.g.,
[
12
]
).

One of the most celebrated results in the theory of perpetuities originates from Kesten’s seminal work
[
25
]
and its subsequent refinements (see, e.g.,
[
12
, Section 4.4]
for an extensive discussion of variants of Kesten’s theorem). The key assumption in these studies is the existence of a positive parameter
\alpha>0
satisfying

\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathbb{E}}[\|\Pi_{n}\|^{\alpha}]^{1/n}=1.

Under this condition—and additional technical assumptions on the distribution of
({\mathbf{A}},\underline{B})
—one can show that there exists a non-null Radon measure
\mu
such that the solution to (
1.2
) fulfills

t^{\alpha}\,{\mathbb{P}}\left(t^{-1}\underline{X}\in\cdot\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mu,\qquad t\to+\infty,

where
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v}}{{\longrightarrow}}
denotes vague convergence of measures.

In the univariate case, the parameter
\alpha>0
is determined by the equation
{\mathbb{E}}[|A|^{\alpha}]=1
. Moreover, under additional assumptions, Kesten’s theorem guarantees that
\lim_{t\to+\infty}t^{\alpha}{\mathbb{P}}(X>t)
exists and is strictly positive. Interestingly, as we explain below, free perpetuity
{\mathbb{X}}
exhibits a similar power-law tail behavior under the condition that
{\mathbb{A}}
has unit mean.

Recent advances in machine learning theory have demonstrated that the heavy-tail behavior observed in Stochastic Gradient Descent can be analyzed within a probabilistic framework using stochastic recursions of the form

\underline{X}_{k}={\mathbf{A}}_{k}\,\underline{X}_{k-1}+\underline{B}_{k},

see
[
17
,
21
,
15
]
. Notably, (
1.2
) characterizes the stationary distribution of the resulting Markov chain.

Our primary motivation for studying (
1.1
) arises from the matricial case:

{\mathbf{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{X}}{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}+{\mathbf{B}},\quad({\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{B}})\mbox{ and }{\mathbf{X}}\mbox{ are independent,}

where
{\mathbf{A}}
,
{\mathbf{B}}
and
{\mathbf{X}}
are
N\times N
random matrix,
{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}={\mathbf{B}}
a.s.
Although one may vectorize this equation and apply classical (vector) perpetuity theory, we propose an approach that preserves the matrix structure. This framework paves the way for studying properties such as the asymptotic behavior of the empirical spectral distribution of
{\mathbf{X}}
as
N\to+\infty
— a topic that, to the best of our knowledge, has not been previously explored.

Before turning to matricial perpetuities, our first goal is to develop the corresponding theory for free random variables. Since many families of independent random matrices are asymptotically free as the dimension of matrices tend to
+\infty
, the free perpetuity naturally emerges as a limiting object. In a forthcoming work, we will investigate the connection between matricial and free perpetuities.

Remark 1.1
.

The assumption
{\mathbb{A}}\geq 0
in (
1.1
) is made without loss of generality. Indeed, starting from the more general equation

{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}\,{\mathbb{X}}\,{\mathbb{A}}^{*}+{\mathbb{B}},

and writing the polar decomposition
{\mathbb{A}}=U|{\mathbb{A}}|
(with
U
unitary), we obtain

{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}U\Bigl(|{\mathbb{A}}|{\mathbb{X}}|{\mathbb{A}}|+U^{*}{\mathbb{B}}\,U\Bigr)U^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}|{\mathbb{A}}|{\mathbb{X}}|{\mathbb{A}}|+U^{*}{\mathbb{B}}\,U,

where the pair
(|{\mathbb{A}}|,U^{*}{\mathbb{B}}\,U)
is
*
-free from
{\mathbb{X}}
. Thus, we may assume
{\mathbb{A}}
is positive and redefine
(|{\mathbb{A}}|,U^{*}{\mathbb{B}}U)\mapsto({\mathbb{A}}^{1/2},{\mathbb{B}})
to obtain (
1.1
).

1.2.
Free multiplicative convolution powers and existence of free perpetuity

Our strategy for proving the existence of the free perpetuity mirrors the classical approach: we analyze the convergence of the series

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}{\mathbb{B}}_{n}\,{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2},

where
\{({\mathbb{A}}_{n},{\mathbb{B}}_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
is an appropriate sequence of free copies.

In our setting, it suffices to establish convergence in distribution; in fact, we prove stronger notions of convergences. Assume that
{\mathcal{A}}

is a von Neumann algebra and
\tau\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a faithful, normal, tracial state. By
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
we denote the algebra of unbounded operators affiliated with
{\mathcal{A}}
.

In the particular case when
{\mathbb{A}}=\alpha\cdot 1_{{\mathcal{A}}}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the series (
1.3
) is a sum of free random variables. In this situation, results from
[
7
]
imply that the series converges almost uniformly (a.u.) provided that

\tau\bigl(\log^{+}|{\mathbb{B}}|\bigr)<+\infty.

Almost uniform convergence is the non-commutative analogue of almost sure convergence.
More generally, if
\tau({\mathbb{A}})<1
, one may show that the series converges in norm when
{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
, and in
L^{1}
if
{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}}
are unbounded operators affiliated with
({\mathcal{A}},\tau)
(with
{\mathbb{B}}\geq 0
and
\tau({\mathbb{B}})<+\infty
). If
\tau({\mathbb{A}})>1
, the perpetuity fails to exist. Thus, the most challenging–and interesting–case is when
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
. Then,
{\mathbb{X}}
necessarily must be unbounded.

A key component in our analysis of the critical case
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
is understanding the asymptotic behavior of moments under free multiplicative convolution. In particular, we prove the following theorem (see Theorems
3.6
and
3.10
for complete statements and proofs). Here, we write
f(x)\sim g(x)
if the quotient

f(x)/g(x)
goes to
1
as
x
tends to
+\infty
. We also denote
m_{\alpha}(\mu)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}t^{\alpha}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}t)
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume that
\mu
is a probability measure on
[0,+\infty)
, which is non-Dirac and has unit mean. Then:

(i)

If for
p\in\mathbb{N}
we have
m_{p}(\mu)<+\infty
, then

m_{p}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})\sim c_{1}\,n^{p-1}.

(ii)

If
m_{2}(\mu)<+\infty
, then for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
,

m_{\gamma}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})\sim c_{2}\,n^{\gamma-1}.

(iii)

If
\mu\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
c>0
, then
m_{2}(\mu)=+\infty
and, for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
,

m_{\gamma}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})\sim c_{3}\,n^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha-1}}.

The constants
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
and
c_{3}
are explicit.

It is worth noting that while the classical multiplicative convolution
\circledast
satisfies

m_{\gamma}(\mu^{\circledast n})=\bigl(m_{\gamma}(\mu)\bigr)^{n},

the free multiplicative convolution exhibits a markedly different asymptotic behavior.

The above theorem allows us to establish existence of free perpetuity in the critical case
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume that
{\mathbb{A}}\geq 0
is non-Dirac,
{\mathbb{B}}
is bounded and that
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
. Then the series (
1.3
)
converges bilaterally almost uniformly. Moreover, if the tail of
\mu_{\mathbb{A}}
satisfies

\mu_{\mathbb{A}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}\quad\text{for some }c>0\text{ and }\alpha\in(1,2),

then the series converges almost uniformly.

1.3.
Subordination

Subordination is a powerful tool in free probability. In its simplest form, if
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
are free self-adjoint random variables, then their Cauchy transforms satisfy

G_{{\mathbb{X}}+{\mathbb{Y}}}(z)=G_{\mathbb{X}}\bigl(\omega(z)\bigr),

for some analytic self-map
\omega
of
{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
. By combining the linearization trick with Voiculescu’s operator-valued subordination results (see
[
5
]
), similar subordination principles hold for general non-commutative polynomials in free variables.

In this work we apply subordination to analyze the tails of the free perpetuity, focusing on the polynomial

{\mathbb{A}}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}+{\mathbb{B}}.

The following theorem generalizes results from
[
27
]
by permitting unbounded operators and removing the assumption that
{\mathbb{A}}
is a function of
{\mathbb{B}}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
,
{\mathbb{A}}
,
{\mathbb{B}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
be three selfadjoint operators affiliated with the tracial
W^{*}
-probability
space
(\mathcal{A},\tau)
, with
{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbb{C}\cdot 1_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Then there exists a pair of analytic self-maps of
\mathbb{C}^{+}
, denoted
(f,\mathsf{f})
, such that

\tau\left((\mathsf{f}(z)-\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)=\frac{1}{\mathsf{f}(z)+f(z)}=\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}.

In addition, the point
(f(z),\mathsf{f}(z))\in\mathbb{C}^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{+}
is the unique attracting fixed point of the map

\mathscr{F}_{z}\colon\mathbb{C}^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{+},\quad\mathscr{F}_{z}\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\tau\left((w_{2}-\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)}-w_{2}\\
\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+w_{1}\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-w_{1}\end{pmatrix}.

Moreover, if
{\mathbb{X}}
is *-free form
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})
, then the functions
f(z),\mathsf{f}(z)
satisfy

\displaystyle\tau\left((\mathsf{f}(z)-\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)=\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{AXA})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right),

\displaystyle\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\right)=\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{AXA})^{-1}\right).

The subordination functions
\mathsf{f}
and
f
associated with the polynomial
{\mathbb{A}}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}+{\mathbb{B}}
play a central role in analyzing the asymptotic behavior of the tails of free perpetuities.

1.4.
Tails of free perpetuities

Once the existence of the free perpetuity in the critical case
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
is established, it is natural to investigate its tail behavior. In this regime the perpetuity necessarily has unbounded support, and subordination becomes an essential tool.
By exploiting the asymptotic properties of the subordination functions, we obtain the following tail estimates.

Theorem 1.5
.

Assume
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
, where
{\mathbb{A}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
is non-Dirac and
{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
, with
{\mathbb{B}}\geq 0
and

{\mathbb{B}}\neq 0
. If
{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbb{C}\cdot 1_{{\mathcal{A}}}
, then the unique solution
{\mathbb{X}}
to
(
1.1
) exists, and moreover:

(i)

if
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})<+\infty
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}t^{1/2}\mu_{{\mathbb{X}}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}}.

(ii)

if
\mu_{\mathbb{A}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
c>0
, then

\lim_{t\to+\infty}t^{1/\alpha}\mu_{{\mathbb{X}}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\frac{\sin(\pi/\alpha)}{\pi/\alpha}\left(\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{\pi\,c}\tau({\mathbb{B}})\right)^{1/\alpha}.

Note that condition
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
plays a similar role to Kesten’s condition for determining the parameter
\alpha
.

A surprising phenomenon emerges from these results. In case (i) the tail of
\mu_{\mathbb{X}}
decays as
t^{-1/2}
(so that
m_{\gamma}({\mathbb{X}})<+\infty
for
\gamma\in(0,1/2)
), whereas in case (ii) when
{\mathbb{A}}
has a regularly varying tail the decay is
t^{-1/\alpha}
, implying
m_{\gamma}({\mathbb{X}})<+\infty
for
\gamma\in(0,1/\alpha)
. Since
\alpha\in(1,2)
, in the second case the free perpetuity
{\mathbb{X}}
possesses more finite moments even though
{\mathbb{A}}
itself has fewer moments. A similar phenomenon occurs in the next theorem, which treats the case when
{\mathbb{B}}
is symmetric.

Theorem 1.6
.

Assume
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
, where
{\mathbb{A}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
is non-Dirac, and
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}({\mathbb{A}},-{\mathbb{B}})
with

{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
. If
{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbb{C}\cdot 1_{{\mathcal{A}}}
, then the unique solution
{\mathbb{X}}
to
(
1.1
) exists, and moreover:

(i)

if
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})<+\infty
, then

\lim_{t\to+\infty}t\,\mu_{|{\mathbb{X}}|}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}}.

(ii)

if
\mu_{\mathbb{A}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
c>0
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}t^{2/\alpha}\,\mu_{|{\mathbb{X}}|}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\frac{\sin(\pi/\alpha)}{\pi/\alpha}\left(\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{2\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})\right)^{1/\alpha}.

1.5.
An explicit example of a perpetuity

Explicit examples of perpetuities—cases in which the density can be expressed in closed form—are rare in both the classical and free settings. To illustrate our theory, we consider an example based on the free beta prime distribution. Following
[
37
]
, define the free beta prime law by

Explicit examples of perpetuities (i.e., cases where the distribution can be written in closed form) are rare in both the classical and free settings. To illustrate our theory, we consider an example based on the free beta prime distribution. Following Yoshida
[
37
]
, define the free beta prime law by

f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,b}(\mathrm{d}x)=f_{a,b}(x)\mathrm{d}x+\max\{1-a,0\}\delta_{0}(\mathrm{d}x),

where

\displaystyle f_{a,b}(x)=\frac{(b-1)\sqrt{(\gamma_{+}-x)(x-\gamma_{-})}}{2\pi x(1+x)}I_{[\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+}]}(x)

with

\gamma_{\pm}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{ab}\pm\sqrt{a+b-1}}{b-1}\right)^{2}.

This formula extends to the case
b=1
, in which case

f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,1}(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{\sqrt{4ax-(a-1)^{2}}}{2\pi x(1+x)}I_{[(a-1)^{2}/(4a),+\infty)}(x)\mathrm{d}x+\max\{1-a,0\}\delta_{0}(\mathrm{d}x).

A direct calculation (e.g., via the corresponding
S
-transforms) shows that for
a>0
,
b\geq 1
, if
{\mathbb{A}}\sim f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,a+b}
and
{\mathbb{X}}\sim f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,b}
(with
{\mathbb{A}}
and
{\mathbb{X}}
free), then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{A}}.

Therefore,
{\mathbb{X}}
is an example of a free perpetuity. Note that while the existence of perpetuity is verified here by a direct calculation, it is nevertheless guaranteed by Theorem
1.3
.

Since
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=a/(a+b-1)<1
if and only if
b>1
, in which case the support of
{\mathbb{X}}
is compact. When
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
, then necessarily
b=1
, and thus
{\mathbb{X}}\sim f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,1}
. In this critical case, one obtains

S_{\mathbb{X}}(-1/x)=\frac{1}{ax-1}\sim\frac{1}{a}\,x^{-1}\quad\text{as }x\to+\infty,

and by
[
26
, Theorem 4.5]
(with
\alpha=1/2
) it follows that

\lim_{t\to+\infty}t^{1/2}\,\mu_{{\mathbb{X}}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\frac{2\sqrt{a}}{\pi}.

A direct computation shows that this expression can be rewritten as

\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{\tau({\mathbb{A}})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}},

which is in full agreement with Theorem
1.5
.

Another explicit example comes from
[
32
]
. For a special choice of parameters in the free GIG distribution (see subsection
4.1
for detailed discussion), suppose
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
are free, where
{\mathbb{X}}
follows a GIG distribution and
{\mathbb{Y}}
follows a free Poisson distribution. Define
\mathbb{V}={\mathbb{X}}^{-1}-({\mathbb{X}}+{\mathbb{Y}})^{-1}
. Then, one obtains
\mathbb{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{Y}}
. Moreover, Hua’s identity implies

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Y}}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{Y}}^{-1}{\mathbb{X}}+{\mathbb{X}},

which is an affine fixed-point equation.

In
[
19
, Theorem 4.1]
, the free GIG distribution is characterized via a continued fraction fixed-point equation:

{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}({\mathbb{X}}+{\mathbb{Y}})^{-1},

where
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
are free and
{\mathbb{Y}}
has a free Poisson distribution. The method of the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 4.1]

heavily relied on subordination techniques. In our work, we also employ subordination; however, we do not use it to establish the existence of a perpetuity.

1.6.
Organization of the paper

The paper is organized as follows. In the next section, we introduce the necessary background and notation. We review several topics, including various modes of convergence in von Neumann algebras, the essential free probability transforms, and key Tauberian theorems. This material provides the theoretical framework required for the later sections.

In Section
3
, we present results on the free multiplicative random walk, a central object in the study of free perpetuities. We analyze the asymptotic behavior of moments and fractional moments of free multiplicative convolutions. We also analyze the conditions for series convergence in the critical case when
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
, laying the groundwork for our fixed point analysis.

Section
4
focuses on the affine fixed point equation. We illustrate these concepts with two examples. In addition, we address the existence and uniqueness of a free perpetuity and study their moments.

In Section
5
, we focus on the subordination phenomenon in the context of the affine transformation
{\mathbb{A}}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}+{\mathbb{B}}
. This section provides key insights into how subordination techniques can be applied to better understand the underlying structure of our model.

Finally, Section
6
investigates the tail behavior of free perpetuities in the critical case, revisiting our two running examples.

An Appendix is included, which presents new results on the limit theorem from
[
31
]
that are not directly related to free perpetuities.

2.
Background and notation

Throughout this paper, we consider the framework of a tracial
W^{*}
non-commutative probability space, by which we mean the a pair

\left({\mathcal{A}},\tau\right)
where
{\mathcal{A}}
is a von Neumann algebra and
\tau\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a faithful, normal, tracial state. By
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
we will denote the algebra of unbounded operators affiliated with
{\mathcal{A}}
.

2.1.
Modes of convergence in von Neumann algebras

One of the central themes of this work is the convergence of sequences of non-commutative random variables. In this subsection, we review the essential definitions and results that underpin our analysis.

In this work we deal with freeness of unbounded operators, for definition of freeness in this case we refer to
[
8
]
or
[
29
, Chapter 8]
.

Definition 2.1
.

(1)

A sequence
(x_{n})_{n}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
converges almost uniformly to
x\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
if, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there is a projection
p
in
{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\tau(1-p)<\varepsilon
and

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|(x_{n}-x)p\|=0.

See
[
23
]
for details.

(2)

A sequence
(x_{n})_{n}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
converges bilaterally almost uniformly to
x\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
if, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there is a projection
p
in
{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\tau(1-p)<\varepsilon
and

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|p(x_{n}-x)p\|=0.

(3)

We say that
(x_{n})_{n}
is Cauchy bilaterally almost uniformly if for any
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
there are a projection
p
with
\tau(1-p)\leq\varepsilon
and a positive integer
N
such that

\|p(x_{n}-x_{m})p\|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}\qquad\mbox{for all }n,m\geq N.

(4)

Let
p\in(0,+\infty)
and
\|x\|_{p}=\tau(|x^{*}x|^{p/2})^{1/p},x\in\mathcal{A}.
It is known (and can easily be verified) that
x\mapsto\|x\|_{p}
is a norm on
\mathcal{A}

if
p\geq 1
and a quasinorm is
p\in(0,1)
. Thus, it generates a topological vector space structure on
\mathcal{A}
. We define
L^{p}(\mathcal{A},\tau)
to be the completion
of
\mathcal{A}
in the topology generated by
\|\cdot\|_{p}
. If
p\geq 1
, then
L^{p}(\mathcal{A},\tau)
is a Banach space (for more details, see
[
34
, Chapter IX]
).

(5)

If
\mathcal{A}
is a von Neumann algebra acting on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
,
the strong operator (so) topology is the locally convex topology on
\mathcal{A}

generated by the family of seminorms
\mathcal{A}\ni x\mapsto\|x\xi\|_{2}
,
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
,
and the weak operator (wo) topology is the locally convex topology on
\mathcal{A}

generated by the family of seminorms
\mathcal{A}\ni x\mapsto|\langle x\xi,\eta\rangle|
,
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
(see
[
33
]
).

It is known that
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
is complete with respect to a.u. and b.a.u. convergences, see

[
13
, Theorem 2.3]
and
[
28
, Theorem 2.2]
. We note that the notion of ‘almost uniform convergence’ is stronger than ‘bilateral almost uniform convergence’, but they coincide in the commutative case. By
[
14
]
, any noncommutative
L^{1}
-bounded martingale converges b.a.u., and any
L^{2}
-bounded martingale converges a.u. A recent article
[
22
]
shows that, in contrast to the classical case, almost uniform convergence can fail for noncommutative
L^{p}
-bounded martingales when
1\leq p<2
.

2.2.
Free probability transforms

Let
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}_{+}
denote the sets of probability measures on
{\mathbb{R}}
and
[0,+\infty)
, respectively. Let
\mathcal{M}_{S}
denote the set of symmetric probability measures on
{\mathbb{R}}
.

The moments and the variance of a measure
\mu
are given by

m_{\alpha}(\mu)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}t^{\alpha}\mu(\mathrm{d}t)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{Var}(\mu)=m_{2}(\mu)-m_{1}(\mu)^{2}.

The moment transform of
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
is defined by

\psi_{\mu}(z)=\int_{[0,+\infty)}\frac{zt}{1-zt}\mu(\mathrm{d}t),\qquad 1/z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mu).

Let
\delta=\mu(\{0\})<1
. If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
, the function
\psi_{\mu}\colon(-\infty,0)\to(\delta-1,0)
is invertible, and we denote its inverse by
\chi_{\mu}
.
If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{S}
, then the function

\psi_{\mu}\colon i(-\infty,0)\to(\delta-1,0)
is invertible, and its inverse is also denoted by
\chi_{\mu}
.

The
S
-transform of
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cup\mathcal{M}_{S}
is defined by

S_{\mu}(z)=\frac{z+1}{z}\chi_{\mu}(z),\qquad z\in(\delta-1,0).

The
S
-transform is multiplicative under the free multiplicative convolution
\boxtimes
, that is,

S_{\mu\boxtimes\nu}=S_{\mu}\,S_{\nu},

where
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\cup\mathcal{M}_{S}
,
[
1
, Theorem 7]
.

If
m_{2}(\mu)<+\infty
, then as
z\to 0-
,

(2.1)

\displaystyle S_{\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{m_{1}(\mu)}-\frac{\mathrm{Var}(\mu)}{m_{1}(\mu)^{3}}z+o(z).

If
\mu
is the distribution of
{\mathbb{X}}
with respect to
\tau
, then we write
\psi_{\mu}=\psi_{\mathbb{X}}
,

\chi_{\mu}=\chi_{\mathbb{X}}
and
S_{\mu}=S_{\mathbb{X}}
.

2.3.
Tauberian theorems

For real functions
f
,
g
defined on a neighbourhood of infinity, we write
f(t)\sim g(t)
if
f(t)/g(t)\to 1
as
t\to+\infty
.
A positive measurable function
L
defined in a neighbourhood of infinity is called slowly varying if
L(\lambda t)\sim L(t)
for each
\lambda>0
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and let
L
be a slowly varying function.

(i)

If
\alpha\in(0,1)
, then the following two conditions are equivalent:

\mu\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim\frac{L(t)}{t^{\alpha}}\qquad\iff\qquad-\psi_{\mu}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)\sim\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}\frac{L(t)}{t^{\alpha}}.

(ii)

If
\alpha\in(1,2)
, then the following two conditions are equivalent:

\mu\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim\frac{L(t)}{t^{\alpha}}\quad\iff\quad-\psi_{\mu}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)=\frac{m_{1}(\mu)}{t}+\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}\frac{L(t)}{t^{\alpha}}(1+o(1)).

Proof.

Let
U_{n}(t)=\int_{[0,t]}x^{n}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)
,
n=1,2
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we write

\displaystyle-\psi_{\mu}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)=\int_{[0,+\infty)}\frac{x}{t+x}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{[0,+\infty)}\frac{\mathrm{d}U_{1}(x)}{t+x}.

If
\alpha\in(1,2)
, then
m_{1}(\mu)<+\infty
and we express
\psi_{\mu}
as

-\psi_{\mu}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)=\frac{m_{1}(\mu)}{t}-\frac{1}{t}\int_{[0,+\infty)}\frac{\mathrm{d}U_{2}(x)}{t+x}.

By
[
10
, Theorem 1.6.4]
, for
0<\alpha<n
, as
t\to+\infty
,

\mu\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim\frac{L(t)}{t^{\alpha}}\qquad\iff\qquad U_{n}(t)\sim\frac{\alpha}{n-\alpha}t^{n-\alpha}L(t).

Moreover, by
[
10
, Theorem 1.7.4]
(with
\rho=1
and
\sigma=n-\alpha
), we have as
t\to+\infty
,

U_{n}(t)\sim\frac{\alpha}{n-\alpha}t^{n-\alpha}L(t)\qquad\iff\qquad\int_{[0,+\infty)}\frac{\mathrm{d}U_{n}(x)}{t+x}\sim c_{n,\alpha}t^{n-\alpha-1}L(t),

where by Euler’s reflection formula, we have

c_{n,\alpha}=\frac{\alpha}{n-\alpha}\Gamma(1-(n-\alpha))\Gamma(n-\alpha+1)=\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi(n-\alpha))}=(-1)^{n+1}\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}.

∎

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{S}
and let
L
be a slowly varying function.
For
\alpha\in(0,2)
, the following two conditions are equivalent: as
t\to+\infty
,

\mu\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim\frac{L(t)}{2\,t^{\alpha}}\qquad\iff\qquad-\psi_{\mu}\left(-\frac{1}{it}\right)\sim\frac{\pi\alpha/2}{\sin(\pi\alpha/2)}\frac{L(t)}{t^{\alpha}}.

Proof.

Let
\mu^{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
be the pushforward measure of
\mu
by the mapping
x\mapsto x^{2}
. Then,

\mu\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim\frac{L(t)}{2\,t^{\alpha}}\qquad\iff\qquad\mu^{2}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim\frac{L(t^{1/2})}{t^{\alpha/2}}.

Since
t\mapsto L(t^{1/2})
is slowly varying and
\alpha/2\in(0,1)
, by Theorem
2.2
(i), we have the equivalence as
t\to+\infty
,

\mu^{2}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim\frac{L(t^{1/2})}{t^{\alpha/2}}\qquad\iff\qquad-\psi_{\mu^{2}}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)\sim\frac{\pi\alpha/2}{\sin(\pi\alpha/2)}\frac{L(t^{1/2})}{t^{\alpha/2}}.

Finally, since
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{S}
, we have

-\psi_{\mu}\left(-\frac{1}{it}\right)=-\psi_{\mu^{2}}\left(-\frac{1}{t^{2}}\right),

which proves the assertion.
∎

3.
Free multiplicative random walk

Assume that
{\mathbb{A}}={\mathbb{A}}^{\ast}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathbb{A}}\geq 0
. Let
\mu
be the distribution of
{\mathbb{A}}
and let
({\mathbb{A}}_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of freely independent copies of
{\mathbb{A}}
. Define

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}^{\uparrow}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}{\mathbb{A}}_{n}{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2},

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\Pi_{n}^{\uparrow}\sim\mu^{\boxtimes n}
.
We will denote by
\Pi_{n}
a generic element with distribution
\mu^{\boxtimes n}
.
We note that if
m_{1}(\mu)=1
, then
(\Pi_{n}^{\uparrow})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a non-commutative martingale,
[
14
]
.

3.1.
Free multiplicative law of large numbers

In the following section, we present several results that describe the asymptotic behavior of the distribution of
\Pi_{n}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
has a compact support.
Let
L_{n}=\sup\left(\mathrm{supp}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})\right)
be the right endpoint of the support of
\Pi_{n}
.
Then,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{L_{n}}{n\,m_{1}(\mu)^{n}}=e\frac{\mathrm{Var}(\mu)}{m_{1}(\mu)^{2}}.

Proof.

By
[
24
, Theorem 1]
, the claim holds for the case
m_{1}(\mu)=1
. The general case can be deduced by applying this result to the dilated measure defined by
\tilde{\mu}(B)=\mu(m_{1}(\mu)B)
for any Borel set
B
. Clearly,
\tilde{\mu}
has a unit mean,
\mathrm{Var}(\tilde{\mu})=\mathrm{Var}(\mu)/m_{1}(\mu)^{2}
and
L_{n}=m_{1}(\mu)^{n}\tilde{L}_{n}
, where
\tilde{L}_{n}=\sup\left(\mathrm{supp}(\tilde{\mu}^{\boxtimes n})\right)
.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
has a compact support and
m_{1}(\mu)<1
, then
L_{n}\to 0
and therefore

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|\Pi_{n}\|=0.

Moreover, in this case, we see that the series

\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\Pi_{n}

converges in norm. Let
{\mathbb{X}}_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Pi_{k}
; one has
\|\Pi_{k}\|\sim c\,k\,m_{1}(\mu)^{k}
. Therefore, for
n>m\geq N
,

\|{\mathbb{X}}_{n}-{\mathbb{X}}_{m}\|\leq\sum_{k=N}^{+\infty}\|\Pi_{k}\|<\varepsilon

for sufficiently large
N
.

The following law of large numbers for free multiplicative convolutions points out a fundamental difference from the usual LLN, where one has almost sure convergence to a constant. Here, the limiting distribution is nontrivial unless
\mu
is Dirac.

Theorem 3.3

(Theorem 2 in
[
18
]
)
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
with
\delta=\mu(\{0\})
. Then,

\mu_{\Pi_{n}^{1/n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\nu,

where
\nu(\{0\})=\delta
and for
t\in(\delta,1)
,

\nu\left(\left[0,\frac{1}{S_{\mu}(t-1)}\right]\right)=t.

Remark 3.4
.

The support of
\nu
is the closure of the interval

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{m_{-1}(\mu)},m_{1}(\mu)\right).

Since
S_{\mu}
is analytic,
\nu
has no atoms except possibly at
0
.

Corollary 3.5
.

If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
m_{1}(\mu)=1
, then

\mu_{\Pi_{n}}=\mu^{\boxtimes n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\delta_{0}.

Indeed, by the previous remark, we have
\nu\big([0,1)\big)=1
.
Therefore, for any
s,t\in(0,1)
and
n\geq\log(t)/\log(s)
, we obtain

\mu_{\Pi_{n}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\mu_{\Pi_{n}^{1/n}}\big((t^{1/n},+\infty)\big)\leq\mu_{\Pi_{n}^{1/n}}\big((s,+\infty)\big)\to\nu\big((s,+\infty)\big)=\nu\big((s,1)\big).

By letting
s\to 1-
, the result follows.

3.2.
Moments

In this subsection we study the asymptotic behavior of free moments of the
n
-fold free multiplicative convolution of a measure
\mu
which has itself
p
-th moment finite. The next subsection studies the analogous problem for fractional moments. The main result of this subsection is the following.

Theorem 3.6
.

Assume that
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
is non-Dirac. For any
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that
m_{p}(\mu)<+\infty
, we have

\lim_{n\to+\infty}n^{1-p}\frac{m_{p}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})}{m_{1}(\mu)^{np}}=\frac{(\eta\,p)^{p-1}}{p!},

where
\eta=\mathrm{Var}(\mu)/m_{1}(\mu)^{2}
.

In order to prove the above result, we will use a combinatorial description of the free multiplicative convolution. For a detailed discussion, we refer to
[
30
]
. Additionally, we note that when
\mu
has compact support, the asymptotics bounds for
m_{p}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})
can be derived from the results of
[
2
]
.

A partition
\pi
of
[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
is a set of subsets of
[n]
of the form
\pi=\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{k}\}
, where
V_{i}\cap V_{j}=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
and
V_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V_{k}=[n]
. The subsets
V_{1},\ldots,V_{k}
are called blocks of
\pi
and we write
V_{i}\in\pi
. Let
|\pi|
denote the number of blocks of
\pi
and
|V_{i}|
the size of the
i
-th block.

We say that
\pi
is a non-crossing partition of
[n]
if for
i_{1},i_{3}\in V
and
i_{2},i_{4}\in V^{\prime}
with
V,V^{\prime}\in\pi
condition
i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}<i_{4}
implies
V=V^{\prime}
. The set of non-crossing partitions on
[n]
is denoted by
{\mathrm{NC}}(n)
. For
\pi,\rho\in{\mathrm{NC}}(n)
, we write
\pi\leq\rho
if every block of
\pi
is contained in some block of
\rho
. The partial order
\leq
is called the reversed refinement order and it defines a lattice structure on
{\mathrm{NC}}(n)
. We denote by
0_{n}
and
1_{n}
respectively the minimal and maximal elements of
{\mathrm{NC}}(n)
with respect to
\leq
.

For
\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(n)
, we define its Kreweras complement
{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)\in{\mathrm{NC}}(n)
as follows:consider set
\{1,\overline{1},2,\overline{2},\ldots,n,\overline{n}\}
, place
\pi
on
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and then
{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)
is the maximal partition on
\{\overline{1},\ldots,\overline{n}\}
such that
\pi\cup{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)
is a non-crossing partition on
\{1,\overline{1},\ldots,n,\overline{n}\}
.

Let
\kappa_{p}
be the free cumulant of order
p\in\mathbb{N}
. For
\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(n)
, we denote

\displaystyle\kappa_{\pi}({\mathbb{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{n})

\displaystyle=\prod_{V\in\pi}\kappa_{|V|}({\mathbb{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{n}|V),

\displaystyle\tau_{\pi}({\mathbb{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{n})

\displaystyle=\prod_{V\in\pi}\tau_{|V|}({\mathbb{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{n}|V).

where for
V=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}
with
i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{k}
, we have
\kappa_{|V|}({\mathbb{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{n}|V)=\kappa_{k}({\mathbb{A}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{i_{k}})
, and
\tau_{|V|}({\mathbb{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{n}|V)=\tau({\mathbb{A}}_{i_{1}}\ldots{\mathbb{A}}_{i_{k}})
. If
{\mathbb{A}}_{1}=\ldots={\mathbb{A}}_{n}={\mathbb{A}}
will use a short hand notation
\kappa_{\pi}({\mathbb{A}})
instead of
\kappa_{\pi}({\mathbb{A}},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}})
.

We will use the following two formulas, see Theorem 14.4 in
[
30
]
. Let
\{{\mathbb{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{n}\}
and
\{{\mathbb{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{B}}_{n}\}
be free families. Then,

(3.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\kappa_{n}({\mathbb{A}}_{1}{\mathbb{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{n}{\mathbb{B}}_{n})=&\sum_{\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(n)}\kappa_{\pi}({\mathbb{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{n})\kappa_{{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)}({\mathbb{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{B}}_{n}),\\
\tau({\mathbb{A}}_{1}{\mathbb{B}}_{1}\ldots{\mathbb{A}}_{n}{\mathbb{B}}_{n})=&\sum_{\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(n)}\kappa_{\pi}({\mathbb{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{n})\tau_{{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)}({\mathbb{B}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{B}}_{n}).\end{split}

We will need a combinatorial formula, which must have been noticed before, however we did not find a good reference and we present it with an easy proof.

Lemma 3.7
.

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{p-1}\frac{r^{r-1}}{r!}\frac{(p-r)^{p-r}}{(p-r)!}=(p-1)\frac{p^{p-1}}{p!},\qquad p\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

Recall Abel’s binomial formula

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{p}\binom{p}{r}(w+r)^{r-1}(z+w+p)^{p-r}=w^{-1}(z+w+p)^{p}

which holds for
w\neq 0
. Set
z=0
. Separating the terms for
r=0
and
r=p
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{p-1}\binom{p}{r}(w+r)^{r-1}(p-r)^{p-r}=\frac{(w+p)^{p}-(p)^{p}}{w}-(w+p)^{p-1}.

Observe that the left-hand side above is a polynomial in
w
. Letting
w\to 0
, we derive the assertion. Alternatively this formula can be proved by a combinatorial argument which goes by using Cayley’s formula, saying that
p^{p-1}
represents the number of rooted labeled trees on
p
vertices.
∎

The following result is easy to establish, e.g., by comparing the coefficients of
z
in the expansions of
S_{\mu^{\boxtimes n}}(z)
and
S_{\mu}^{n}(z)
(recall (
2.1
)).

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
with
m_{2}(\mu)<+\infty
. Then,

\mathrm{Var}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})=n\,\mathrm{Var}(\mu)\,m_{1}(\mu)^{2(n-1)},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}_{1},{\mathbb{A}}_{2}\ldots
be freely independent copies of
{\mathbb{A}}
and assume
{\mathbb{A}}\geq 0
. For any
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{p})<+\infty
, we have

(3.2)

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa_{p}(\Pi_{n})}{n^{p-1}\tau({\mathbb{A}})^{p\,n}}=\frac{(\eta\,p)^{p-1}}{p!}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right),

where
\eta=\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})/\tau({\mathbb{A}})^{2}
.

Proof.

For
p=1
, the assertion holds trivially, while for
p=2
it follows directly from Corollary
3.8
. In both cases the
O(1/n)
term is equal to zero. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
, implying
\eta=\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})=\kappa_{2}({\mathbb{A}})
.

Fix
m\geq 3
and assume that
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{m})<+\infty
. Suppose that (
3.2
) holds for all
p=1,\ldots,m-1
; we aim to prove it for
p=m
.

By traciality,
\kappa_{m}(\Pi_{k})=\kappa_{m}({\mathbb{A}}_{k}\Pi_{k-1})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Applying formula (
3.1
) and using the fact that all
{\mathbb{A}}_{i}
have the same distribution as
{\mathbb{A}}
, we obtain for any
k\geq 2
,

(3.3)

\displaystyle\kappa_{m}(\Pi_{k})-\kappa_{m}(\Pi_{k-1})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(m)\\
|{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)|=2\end{subarray}}\kappa_{\pi}({\mathbb{A}})\kappa_{{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)}(\Pi_{k-1})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(m)\\
|{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)|>2\end{subarray}}\kappa_{\pi}({\mathbb{A}})\kappa_{{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)}(\Pi_{k-1}).

By the induction hypothesis, for any
p<m
, there exists
C_{p}>0
such that for all
k\geq 2
,

|\kappa_{p}(\Pi_{k-1})|\leq k^{p-1}C_{p}
.
Consequently, for any
\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(m)
with
|{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)|>2
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\kappa_{{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)}(\Pi_{k-1})\right|\leq k^{m-|{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)|}\prod_{V\in{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)}C_{|V|}=O(k^{m-3}).

and therefore the second sum in (
3.3
) is of order
O(k^{m-3})
.

When
|{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)|=2
, the partition
\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(m)
contains one block that is a pair and
m-2
singletons.
The value of
\kappa_{{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)}(\Pi_{k-1})
depends on the distance between the two elements in the pair. Specifically, if
i<j
and
\{i,j\}
is the unique pair of
\pi
with
j-i=r
, we have
\kappa_{{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)}(\Pi_{k-1})=\kappa_{r}(\Pi_{k-1})\kappa_{m-r}(\Pi_{k-1})
. There are
m-r
such pairs
\{i,j\}
for
r\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
. Therefore,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(m)\\
|{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)|=2\end{subarray}}\kappa_{\pi}({\mathbb{A}})\kappa_{{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)}(\Pi_{k-1})=\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}(m-r)\kappa_{2}({\mathbb{A}})\kappa_{r}(\Pi_{k-1})\kappa_{m-r}(\Pi_{k-1}).

By the induction hypothesis, for
r=1,\ldots,m-1
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{r}(\Pi_{k-1})

\displaystyle=(k-1)^{r-1}\frac{(\eta\,r)^{r-1}}{r!}+O(k^{r-2}),

\displaystyle\kappa_{m-r}(\Pi_{k-1})

\displaystyle=(k-1)^{m-r-1}\frac{(\eta\,(m-r))^{m-r-1}}{(m-r)!}+O(k^{m-r-2}).

Substituting these into (
3.4
) gives

\displaystyle(k-1)^{m-2}\eta^{m-1}\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}\frac{r^{r-1}}{r!}\frac{(m-r)^{m-r}}{(m-r)!}+O(k^{m-3}).

By Lemma
3.7
, this simplifies to

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(m)\\
|{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)|=2\end{subarray}}\kappa_{\pi}({\mathbb{A}})\kappa_{{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)}(\Pi_{k-1})=(k-1)^{m-2}(m-1)\frac{(\eta\,m)^{m-1}}{m!}+O(k^{m-3}).

Summing (
3.3
) over
k=2,\ldots,n
, we obtain

\displaystyle\kappa_{m}(\Pi_{n})-\kappa_{m}({\mathbb{A}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=2}^{n}\left((k-1)^{m-2}(m-1)\frac{(\eta\,m)^{m-1}}{m!}+O(k^{m-3})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\eta\,m)^{m-1}}{m!}(m-1)\sum_{k=2}^{n}(k-1)^{m-2}+O(n^{m-2}).

Using the asymptotic formula
(m-1)\sum_{k=2}^{n}(k-1)^{m-2}=n^{m-1}+O(n^{m-2})
, the result follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.6
.

The assertion follows easily from Proposition
3.9
. As before, without loss of generality, we assume that
m_{1}(\mu)=1
. Let
{\mathbb{A}}_{1},{\mathbb{A}}_{2}\ldots
be freely independent copies of
{\mathbb{A}}\sim\mu
. We have

\displaystyle m_{p}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})

\displaystyle=\tau(\Pi_{n}^{p})=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(p)}\kappa_{\pi}(\Pi_{n})=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(p)}\prod_{V\in\pi}\kappa_{|V|}(\Pi_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(p)}\prod_{V\in\pi}\left(\frac{\left(\eta\,|V|\right)^{|V|-1}}{|V|!}n^{|V|-1}+O(n^{|V|-2})\right),

where the last equality follows from (
3.2
).
For each fixed
p
, this is a sum over a finite set. The dominating term in
n
in this sum is the one corresponding to
\pi=1_{p}
, and is given by
(\eta\,p)^{p-1}/p!\,n^{p-1}
. All other terms are of smaller order. This completes the proof.
∎

3.3.
Fractional moments

In this section, we find the asymptotics of
m_{\gamma}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})=\tau(\Pi_{n}^{\gamma})
for
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. These asymptotics differ drastically from the case of classical multiplicative convolution
\circledast
, where we have
m_{\gamma}(\mu^{\circledast n})=m_{\gamma}(\mu)^{n}
. Note that if
m_{1}(\mu)=1
, and
\mu
is non-Dirac, then by the strict Jensen inequality, we have
m_{\gamma}(\mu)<1
for all
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

The main result in this section is the following.

Theorem 3.10
.

Assume that
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
is non-Dirac and
m_{1}(\mu)=1
.

(i)

For any
\gamma\in(0,1)
,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}n^{1-\gamma}m_{\gamma}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})=\frac{(\mathrm{Var}(\mu)\gamma)^{\gamma-1}}{\Gamma(1+\gamma)}.

(ii)

For
\gamma_{n}=1/\log(n)
,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{n}{\log(n)}m_{\gamma_{n}}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})=\frac{e}{\mathrm{Var}(\mu)},

If
m_{2}(\mu)=+\infty
, then the limits in (i) and (ii) equal
0
.

(iii)

If
\mu\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(1,2)
, then
\mathrm{Var}(\mu)=+\infty
and, for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}n^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\alpha-1}}m_{\gamma}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})=\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi\gamma}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-\gamma}{\alpha-1}\right)\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\left(\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{\pi\alpha\,c}\right)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\alpha-1}}.

Remark 3.11
.

Observe that the asymptotic from
(i)
in the above theorem is very similar to the analogous asymptotic found for integer moments in Theorem
3.6

The proof of the above theorem will be based on a couple of lemmas. The following result generalizes
[
18
, Lemma 10]
, which assumed that
\mu(\{0\})=0
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
with
\delta=\mu(\{0\})<1
. If
m_{\gamma}(\mu)<+\infty
for
\gamma\in(0,1)
, then

\int_{[0,+\infty)}x^{\gamma}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi\gamma}\int_{0}^{1-\delta}\left(\frac{1-t}{t}\frac{1}{S_{\mu}(-t)}\right)^{\gamma}\mathrm{d}t.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}y^{-\gamma}\psi^{\prime}_{\mu}(-y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,+\infty)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{y^{-\gamma}x}{(1+yx)^{2}}\mathrm{d}y\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,+\infty)}x^{\gamma}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{u^{\gamma}(1+u)^{2}}\mathrm{d}u\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi\gamma}{\sin(\pi\gamma)}\int_{[0,+\infty)}x^{\gamma}\mu(\mathrm{d}x),

where in the first equality we have used Tonelli’s theorem, and in the second equality we made the substitution
u=yx
. The third equality follows from the fact that
\int_{0}^{+\infty}u^{-\gamma}(1+u)^{-2}\mathrm{d}u=\pi\gamma/\sin(\pi\gamma)
for
\gamma\in(-1,1)
.

Next, in the initial integral, we substitute
y=-\chi_{\mu}(-t)
. Since
\chi_{\mu}\colon(\delta-1,0)\to(-\infty,0)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}y^{-\gamma}\psi^{\prime}_{\mu}(-y)\mathrm{d}y=\int_{0}^{1-\delta}(-\chi_{\mu}(-t))^{-\gamma}\mathrm{d}t.

The assertion follows from the definition of the
S
-transform of
\mu
.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

For
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and
y>0
, define

\kappa(y)=\int_{[0,+\infty)}(1+yx)^{-1}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)
and
\mu_{y}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
by

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mu_{y}(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{1}{\kappa(y)}\frac{1}{1+yx}\mu(\mathrm{d}x).

(i)

If
\mu
is non-Dirac, then the function
(0,+\infty)\ni y\mapsto m_{1}(\mu_{y})
is strictly decreasing.

(ii)

If
m_{1}(\mu)<+\infty
, then

m_{1}(\mu_{y})=m_{1}(\mu)-\frac{y}{1+y\,m_{1}(\mu)}\int_{[0,+\infty)}(x-m_{1}(\mu))^{2}\mu_{y}(\mathrm{d}x).

(a)

If
m_{2}(\mu)<+\infty
, then

\lim_{y\to 0+}\frac{m_{1}(\mu_{y})-m_{1}(\mu)}{y}=-\mathrm{Var}(\mu).

(b)

If
m_{2}(\mu)=+\infty
, then

\lim_{y\to 0+}\frac{m_{1}(\mu_{y})-m_{1}(\mu)}{y}=-\infty.

(c)

If
\mu\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
c>0
, then as
y\to 0+
,

m_{1}(\mu_{y})=m_{1}(\mu)+\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}c\,y^{\alpha-1}(1+o(1)).

Remark 3.14
.

Note that we have
\kappa(y)=1+\psi_{\mu}(-y)
.

Moreover, the measures defined in (
3.6
) constitute a Cauchy-Stieltjes kernel family in the sense of
[
11
]
. Point (i) of Lemma
3.13
follows from
[
11
, Section 2]
; we include its proof for completeness.

Proof of Lemma
3.13
.

We have

m_{1}(\mu_{y})=\frac{1}{\kappa(y)}\int_{[0,+\infty)}\frac{x}{1+yx}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{1-\kappa(y)}{y\,\kappa(y)}.

Clearly, the function
(0,+\infty)\ni y\mapsto m_{1}(\nu_{y})
is differentiable, and we find that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}m_{1}(\mu_{y})=\frac{\kappa(y)^{2}-\kappa(y)-y\,\kappa^{\prime}(y)}{y^{2}\kappa(y)^{2}}.

Since
\mu
is non-degenerate, we apply the strict Jensen inequality to obtain

\kappa(y)^{2}-\kappa(y)-y\,\kappa^{\prime}(y)=\left(\int_{[0,+\infty)}\frac{1}{(1+yx)}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{2}-\int_{[0,+\infty)}\frac{1}{(1+yx)^{2}}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)<0,

which proves (i).

If
m_{1}(\mu)<+\infty
, we have

m_{1}(\mu_{y})-m_{1}(\mu)=\frac{1}{\kappa(y)}\int_{[0,+\infty)}\frac{x-m_{1}(\mu)}{1+yx}\mu(\mathrm{d}x).

Moreover,

\frac{x-m_{1}(\mu)}{1+xy}=\frac{x-m_{1}(\mu)}{1+y\,m_{1}(\mu)}-\frac{y}{1+y\,m_{1}(\mu)}\frac{(x-m_{1}(\mu))^{2}}{1+xy}.

Points (a) and (b) are immediate consequences of the formula stated in (ii).

For (c),
y\to 0+
, we have

m_{1}(\mu)-m_{1}(\mu_{y})\sim\frac{y}{\kappa(y)}\int_{[0,+\infty)}(x-m_{1}(\mu))^{2}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\sim\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}U(x)}{y^{-1}+x},

where
U(x)=\int_{[0,x]}(t-m_{1}(\mu))^{2}\mu(\mathrm{d}t)
. Let
U_{2}(x)=\int_{[0,x]}t^{2}\mu(\mathrm{d}t)
. As in the proof of Theorem
2.2
, we find that as
t\to+\infty
,

\mu\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}\qquad\iff\qquad U_{2}(t)\sim c\frac{\alpha}{2-\alpha}t^{2-\alpha}.

Since
U_{2}(t)\sim U(t)
, we again apply the argument from the proof of Theorem
2.2
to obtain, as
y\to 0+
,

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}U(x)}{y^{-1}+x}\sim-\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}c\,y^{\alpha-1}.

∎

Now, we are ready to prove Theorem
3.10
.

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

(i) Let
\delta=\mu(\{0\})
. By
[
3
, Theorem 4.1]
, we have
\mu_{\Pi_{n}}(\{0\})=\delta
.
Since
S_{\Pi_{n}}=S_{\mu}^{n}
, by Lemma
3.12
,
we obtain for
\gamma\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle m_{\gamma}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi\gamma}\int_{0}^{1-\delta}\left(\frac{1-t}{t}\frac{1}{S_{\mu}(-t)^{n}}\right)^{\gamma}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi\gamma}\int_{0}^{1-\delta}\left(\frac{t}{1-t}\frac{1}{(-\chi_{\mu}(-t))}\right)^{(n-1)\gamma}(-\chi_{\mu}(-t))^{-\gamma}\mathrm{d}t

(3.7)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi\gamma}\int_{[0,+\infty)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{-\psi_{\mu}(-y)\frac{1}{y}}{1+\psi_{\mu}(-y)}\right)^{(n-1)\gamma}y^{-\gamma}\frac{x}{(1+xy)^{2}}\mathrm{d}y\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x),

where we have substituted
(0,1-\delta)\ni t\mapsto-\chi_{\mu}(-t)=y\in(0,+\infty)
and used the fact that
\psi_{\mu}^{\prime}(-y)=\int_{[0,+\infty)}\frac{x}{(1+xy)^{2}}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)
.
Let

\displaystyle m(y)=\frac{-\psi_{\mu}(-y)\frac{1}{y}}{1+\psi_{\mu}(-y)}.

We have
m(y)=m_{1}(\mu_{y})
, where
\mu_{y}
is defined by (
3.6
). Consequently, the results of Lemma
3.13
are applicable.

To prove parts (i) and (ii), we consider a more general situation: if
\gamma_{n}\to\gamma_{\ast}\in[0,1)
with
\gamma_{n}n\to+\infty
as
n\to+\infty
, we will evaluate the limit
\lim_{n\to+\infty}\gamma_{n}n^{1-\gamma_{n}}\tau(\Pi_{n}^{\gamma_{n}})
. We consider (
3.7
) with
\gamma=\gamma_{n}
.

For
s>0
, we substitute
y=s/(\gamma_{n}n)
in (
3.7
). This leads us to analyze the pointwise limit of the integrand function multiplied by
\gamma_{n}n^{1-\gamma_{n}}
:

\displaystyle I_{n}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n}n^{1-\gamma_{n}}\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma_{n})}{\pi\gamma_{n}}m\left(\frac{s}{\gamma_{n}n}\right)^{(n-1)\gamma_{n}}\left(\frac{s}{\gamma_{n}n}\right)^{-\gamma_{n}}\frac{x}{\left(1+\frac{s}{\gamma_{n}n}x\right)^{2}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{n}n}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma_{n})}{\pi\gamma_{n}^{1-\gamma_{n}}}m\left(\frac{s}{\gamma_{n}n}\right)^{(n-1)\gamma_{n}}s^{-\gamma_{n}}\frac{x}{\left(1+\frac{s}{\gamma_{n}n}x\right)^{2}}.

If
m_{2}(\mu)<+\infty
, then by Lemma
3.13
(ii), we obtain

m(y)=1-\mathrm{Var}(\mu)y(1+o(1)),\qquad y\to 0+.

If
m_{2}(\mu)=+\infty
, then again by Lemma
3.13
(ii)

\lim_{y\to 0+}\frac{m(y)-1}{y}=-\infty.

If
m_{2}(\mu)<+\infty
, we obtain, for any
s>0
and
x\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}I_{n}(s,x)

\displaystyle=d_{\gamma_{\ast}}x\,s^{-\gamma_{\ast}}\lim_{n\to+\infty}\left(1-\mathrm{Var}(\mu)\frac{s}{\gamma_{n}n}(1+o(1))\right)^{(n-1)\gamma_{n}}

\displaystyle=d_{\gamma_{\ast}}x\,s^{-\gamma_{\ast}}e^{-\mathrm{Var}(\mu)s}=:I_{\infty}(s,x),

where
d_{\gamma}=\sin(\pi\gamma)/(\pi\gamma^{1-\gamma})
if
\gamma\in(0,1)
, and
d_{0}=1
. If
m_{2}(\mu)=+\infty
, then the above pointwise limit is
0
.

Fix
c\in(0,\mathrm{Var}(\mu))
. Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
m(y)\leq 1-c\,y
for all
y\in(0,\varepsilon]
.
Using the inequality
1-x\leq e^{-x}
for
x\geq 0
, we obtain for
s\leq\varepsilon\gamma_{n}n
,

m\left(\frac{s}{\gamma_{n}n}\right)^{(n-1)\gamma_{n}}\leq\left(1-c\frac{s}{\gamma_{n}n}\right)^{(n-1)\gamma_{n}}\leq e^{-c_{1}s}

for some
c_{1}>0
. Since
\lim_{n\to+\infty}\gamma_{n}=\gamma_{\ast}<1
, we have
\gamma_{n}\leq 1-\tilde{\delta}
for some
\tilde{\delta}\in(0,1)
for large
n
. Therefore, for large
n
, we obtain

I_{n}(s,x)I_{(0,\varepsilon\gamma_{n}n]}(s)\leq c_{2}e^{-c_{1}s}(s^{-(1-\tilde{\delta})}I_{(0,1]}(s)+I_{(1,+\infty)}(s))x\in L^{1}(\mathrm{d}s\times\mu(\mathrm{d}x))

for some positive constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
. The Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem implies that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{[0,+\infty)}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon\gamma_{n}n}I_{n}(s,x)\mathrm{d}y\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{[0,+\infty)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}I_{\infty}(s,x)\mathrm{d}y\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x).

Since
y\mapsto m(y)
is strictly decreasing and
m(\varepsilon)<m(0)=1
for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have for
s>\varepsilon\gamma_{n}n
,

m\left(\frac{s}{\gamma_{n}n}\right)^{(n-1)\gamma}\leq m(\varepsilon)^{(n-1)\gamma}\leq e^{-c_{3}\gamma_{n}n}

for some positive constant
c_{3}
.
Therefore, for
s>\varepsilon\gamma_{n}n
, using the inequality
x(1+\frac{s}{\gamma_{n}n}x)^{-2}\leq\gamma_{n}n/s
, we arrive at

I_{n}(s,x)I_{[\varepsilon\gamma_{n}n,+\infty)}(s)\leq c_{4}e^{-c_{3}n}s^{-1-\gamma}\gamma_{n}n.

for positive
c_{3}
and
c_{4}
.
Thus,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{[0,+\infty)}\int_{\varepsilon\gamma_{n}n}^{+\infty}I_{n}(s,x)\mathrm{d}y\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=0.

Finally, we obtain in the case
m_{2}(\mu)<+\infty
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\gamma_{n}n^{1-\gamma_{n}}m_{\gamma_{n}}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})

\displaystyle=d_{\gamma^{\ast}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}s^{-\gamma_{\ast}}e^{-\mathrm{Var}(\mu)s}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=d_{\gamma^{\ast}}\Gamma(1-\gamma_{\ast})\mathrm{Var}(\mu)^{\gamma_{\ast}-1}.

The same limit equals
0
if
m_{2}(\mu)=+\infty
.
If
\gamma_{\ast}=0
, then the right-hand side above equals
\mathrm{Var}(\mu)^{-1}
.
If
\gamma_{n}=1/\log(n)
, we have

n^{1-\gamma_{n}}\sim e^{-1}n
, thus recovering (ii).
If
\gamma_{n}=\gamma\in(0,1)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\lim_{n\to+\infty}n^{1-\gamma}m_{\gamma}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})=\frac{1}{\gamma}\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi\gamma^{1-\gamma}}\Gamma(1-\gamma)\mathrm{Var}(\mu)^{\gamma-1}.

By Euler’s reflection formula, we obtain

\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi\gamma}\Gamma(1-\gamma)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1+\gamma)},

which proves (i).

For (iii), we substitute
y=s/n^{1/(\alpha-1)}
in (
3.7
) and analyze the integrand function multiplied by
n^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\alpha-1}}
, i.e.,

I_{n}(s,x)=\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi\gamma}m\left(\frac{s}{n^{1/(\alpha-1)}}\right)^{(n-1)\gamma}s^{-\gamma}\frac{x}{\left(1+\frac{s}{n^{1/(\alpha-1)}}x\right)^{2}}.

Since, by Lemma
3.13
, we have

m\left(\frac{s}{n^{1/(\alpha-1)}}\right)=1+\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}c\frac{s^{\alpha-1}}{n}(1+o(1)),

we obtain for any
s>0
and
x\geq 0
,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}I_{n}(s,x)=\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi\gamma}x\,s^{-\gamma}\exp\left(\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}cs^{\alpha-1}\right)=I_{\infty}(s,x).

Bounding as before, we have

\displaystyle I_{n}(s,x)I_{(0,\varepsilon n^{1/(\alpha-1)}]}(s)

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}s^{-\gamma}e^{-c_{1}s^{\alpha-1}}x

and

\displaystyle I_{n}(s,x)I_{(\varepsilon n^{1/(\alpha-1)},+\infty)}(s)

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}e^{-c_{3}n}s^{-1-\gamma}n^{1/(1-\alpha)}

for positive constants
c_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,4
. Thus, by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}n^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\alpha-1}}m_{\gamma}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,+\infty)}\int_{0}^{+\infty}I_{\infty}(s,x)\mathrm{d}s\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\pi\gamma)}{\pi\gamma}\Gamma\left(\frac{1-\gamma}{\alpha-1}\right)\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\left(\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{\pi\alpha\,c}\right)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\alpha-1}}.

∎

3.4.
Series convergence for
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1

Recall that we denote

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}^{\uparrow}={\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}{\mathbb{A}}_{n}{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}

and that
\Pi_{n}
is a generic element with distribution
\mu^{\boxtimes n}
.
Note that if
m_{1}(\mu)=1
, then
(\Pi_{n}^{\uparrow})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a non-commutative martingale. We exploit this fact to establish bilaterall almost uniform convergence of
(\Pi_{n}^{\uparrow})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
The following theorem is the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.15
.

Assume that
{\mathbb{A}}\geq 0
is non-Dirac and
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
. Then, the series

\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\Pi_{n}^{\uparrow}\mbox{ converges b.a.u.}

If additionally
\mu_{\mathbb{A}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
for
c>0
and
\alpha\in(1,2)
, then

\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\Pi_{n}\mbox{ converges a.u.}

Surprisingly, the proof of the latter result above is significantly simpler than the proof of b.a.u. convergence.

We start with a series of lemmas. The first one is the analog of the maximal inequality for martingales.

Lemma 3.16

(Proposition 5 in
[
14
]
)
.

Assume that
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is a nonnegative martingale, and let
M
be a positive number. There exists a projection
q
such that

\tau(1-q)\leq M^{-1}\tau(X_{0})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|q\,X_{n}\,q\|\leq M\mbox{ for all n}.

The proof of the following lemma is based on the proof of
[
14
, Proposition 6]
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
. Assume that
m_{1}(\mu)=1
,
m_{2}(\mu)<+\infty
and that
\mu
is non-Dirac.
Let
(n_{k})_{k}
be an arbitrary monotone increasing positive integer sequence, and let
(h_{k})_{k}
be a sequence of nonnegative numbers such that

\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}e^{-h_{k}}<+\infty.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a projection
p
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\tau(1-p)\leq\varepsilon
and

(3.8)

\displaystyle\|p\,\Pi_{n}^{\uparrow}\,p\|\leq\left(\frac{\log(n_{k})}{n_{k}}\right)^{\log(n_{k})}n_{k}^{h_{k}},\qquad n_{k}\leq n<n_{k+1},\quad k\geq N.

Proof.

Denote
f_{k}=\left(\frac{\log(n_{k})}{n_{k}}\right)^{\log(n_{k})}n_{k}^{h_{k}}
and define
p_{k}=e_{[0,f_{k}]}(\Pi_{n_{k}}^{\uparrow})
.
For
k\geq 1
, let
X^{(k)}_{n}:=p_{k}\,\Pi_{n}^{\uparrow}\,p_{k}
for
n\geq n_{k}
.
Note that for each
k
,
(X^{(k)}_{n})_{n\geq n_{k}}
is a nonnegative martingale. Therefore, by Lemma
3.16
,
for each
k
and any
M_{k}>0
there exists a projection
q_{k}
such that

\tau(1-q_{k})\leq M_{k}^{-1}\tau(X_{n_{k}}^{(k)})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|q_{k}X_{n}^{(k)}q_{k}\|\leq M_{k}\mbox{ for }n\geq n_{k}.

Let
M_{k}=f_{k}
and define

p=\bigwedge_{k\geq N}(q_{k}\wedge p_{k})
, where
N
is yet to be determined.

For all
n\geq n_{k}
and
k\geq N
, we have

\|p\,\Pi_{n}^{\uparrow}\,p\|\leq\|q_{k}p_{k}\Pi_{n}^{\uparrow}p_{k}q_{k}\|=\|q_{k}X_{n}^{(k)}q_{k}\|\leq f_{k}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\tau(1-p)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=N}^{+\infty}\tau(1-q_{k})+\sum_{k=N}^{+\infty}\tau(1-p_{k})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=N}^{+\infty}f_{k}^{-1}\tau(\Pi_{n_{k}}^{\uparrow}p_{k})+\sum_{k=N}^{+\infty}\mu_{\Pi_{n_{k}}}\big((f_{k},+\infty)\big)

By Markov’s inequality, we have

\mu_{\Pi_{n}}\big((f,+\infty)\big)\leq\frac{\tau(\Pi_{n}^{1/\log(n)})}{f^{1/\log(n)}}.

Similarly, since
t\,I_{[0,f]}(t)\leq t\left(f/t\right)^{1-1/\log(n)}=t^{1/\log(n)}f^{1-1/\log(n)}
for
s,t>0
, it follows that

f^{-1}\tau\left(\Pi_{n}e_{[0,f]}(\Pi_{n})\right)=f^{-1}\int_{[0,+\infty)}t\,I_{[0,f]}(t)\,\mu_{\Pi_{n}}(\mathrm{d}t)\leq\frac{\tau(\Pi_{n}^{1/\log(n)})}{f^{1/\log(n)}}.

By (
3.5
), we have

\tau(\Pi_{n}^{1/\log(n)})\leq c\frac{\log(n)}{n}
for
n>1
, where
c
is a positive constant.
Therefore, we can conclude that

\tau(1-p)\leq 2\sum_{k=N}^{+\infty}c\frac{\log(n_{k})}{n_{k}}f_{k}^{-1/\log(n_{k})}=2c\sum_{k=N}^{+\infty}e^{-h_{k}}.

Clearly, one can adjust
N
so that
\tau(1-p)\leq\varepsilon
.
∎

Now, we are ready to prove Theorem
3.15
.

Proof of Theorem
3.15
.

Let
{\mathbb{X}}_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Pi_{k}^{\uparrow}
.
Let
n_{k}=k^{2}
and
h_{k}=\frac{3}{2}\log(k)
. Then, we have
\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}e^{-h_{k}}=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}k^{-3/2}<+\infty
. By applying Lemma
3.17
, there exists a projection
p
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that the inequality (
3.8
) holds.

Let
K\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy
K\geq N
. For
n>m\geq n_{K}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|p({\mathbb{X}}_{n}-{\mathbb{X}}_{m})p\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}\|p\,\Pi_{k}^{\uparrow}\,p\|\leq\sum_{k=K}^{+\infty}\sum_{l=n_{k}}^{n_{k+1}-1}\|p\,\Pi_{l}^{\uparrow}\,p\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=K}^{+\infty}(n_{k+1}-n_{k})\left(\frac{\log(n_{k})}{n_{k}}\right)^{\log(n_{k})}n_{k}^{h_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=K}^{+\infty}(2k+1)\left(\frac{2\log(k)}{k^{2}}\right)^{2\log(k)}k^{3\log(k)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=K}^{+\infty}(2k+1)k^{2\log\log(k)}\left(\frac{4}{k}\right)^{\log(k)}.

It is straightforward to see that this series converges. Therefore, for any
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, we can choose
K
such that
\|p({\mathbb{X}}_{n}-{\mathbb{X}}_{m})p\|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
. This implies that
({\mathbb{X}}_{n})_{n}
is Cauchy bilaterally almost uniformly, which proves the first part of the assertion.

For the second part, we will not rely on martingales.
Let
{\mathbb{X}}_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Pi_{k}
.
For
\beta>0
define
p_{k}=e_{[0,k^{-\beta}]}(\Pi_{k})
and
p=\bigwedge_{k\geq N}p_{k}
.
Fix
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
. Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we find that for
n>m>N
,

\|({\mathbb{X}}_{n}-{\mathbb{X}}_{m})p\|\leq\sum_{k=N}^{\infty}\|\Pi_{k}p_{k}\|\leq\sum_{k=N}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{\beta}}<\varepsilon

for sufficiently large
N
, provided
\beta>1
. Moreover, for
\gamma\in(0,1)
, we have

\tau(1-p)\leq\sum_{k=N}^{+\infty}\tau(1-p_{k})=\sum_{k=N}^{+\infty}\mu_{\Pi_{k}}\big((k^{-\beta},+\infty)\big)\leq\sum_{k=N}^{\infty}k^{\beta\gamma}\tau(\Pi_{k}^{\gamma}).

By Theorem
3.10
(iii), we know that
\tau(\Pi_{k}^{\gamma})\leq Ck^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{\alpha-1}}
for some positive constant
C
. Setting
c:=\frac{2-\alpha}{1+\beta(\alpha-1)}\in(0,1)
, if
\gamma\in(0,c)
, then

\frac{1-\gamma}{\alpha-1}-\beta\gamma>1
. Thus, by increasing
N
if necessary, we obtain
\tau(1-p)\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
∎

4.
Fixed point equations

In this section, we focus on solutions to the affine fixed-point equations of the form

(4.1)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{\ast}+{\mathbb{B}},\qquad({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\mbox{ and }{\mathbb{X}}\mbox{ are *-free,}

where
{\mathbb{B}}={\mathbb{B}}^{\ast}
.
For a given pair
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{2}
, our goal is to establish the existence and uniqueness of the solution and to investigate the properties of this solution.
It is important to note that it is the distribution of
{\mathbb{X}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
that serves as a solution to the above problem, rather than
{\mathbb{X}}
itself.
However, for simplicity, we may occasionally refer to
{\mathbb{X}}
as the solution.

We begin by examining two illustrative examples.

4.1.
Examples

4.1.1.
Free Beta prime distribution

Yoshida
[
37
]
defined the free-beta prime distribution
f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,b}
as the free multiplicative convolution
\mu_{a}\boxtimes\mu_{b}^{-1}
,
a>0,b>1
, where
\mu_{b}^{-1}
is the pushforward measure of
\mu_{b}
by the mapping
x\mapsto x^{-1}
, and
\mu_{\lambda}
is the Marchenko-Pastur distribution defined by

\mu_{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{\sqrt{(\lambda_{+}-x)(x-\lambda_{-})}}{2\pi x}\mathrm{d}x+\max\{1-\lambda,0\}\delta_{0}(\mathrm{d}x),

where
\lambda_{\pm}=(1\pm\sqrt{\lambda})^{2}
,
\lambda>0
. Yoshida obtained the following expression for the free-beta prime distribution:

f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,b}(\mathrm{d}x)=f_{a,b}(x)\mathrm{d}x+\max\{1-a,0\}\delta_{0}(\mathrm{d}x),

where

(4.2)

\displaystyle f_{a,b}(x)=\frac{(b-1)\sqrt{(\gamma_{+}-x)(x-\gamma_{-})}}{2\pi x(1+x)}I_{[\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+}]}(x)

and

\gamma_{\pm}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{ab}\pm\sqrt{a+b-1}}{b-1}\right)^{2}.

We observe that
f_{a,b}
has a point-wise limit as
b\to 1+
, which allows us to extend the definition of
f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,b}
to the case
b=1
. We define

f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,1}(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{\sqrt{4ax-(a-1)^{2}}}{2\pi x(1+x)}I_{[(a-1)^{2}/(4a),+\infty)}(x)\mathrm{d}x+\max\{1-a,0\}\delta_{0}(\mathrm{d}x).

The case of
b=1
was not covered in
[
37
]
. Note that, in contrast to the case
b>1
, for
b=1
, the support of free beta-prime is unbounded.

The
S
-transform of
f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,b}
is
S_{a,b}(z)=S_{\mu_{a}}(z)S_{\mu_{b}^{-1}}(z)=\frac{b-1-z}{a+z}
.

Corollary 4.1
.

Let
a>0
,
b\geq 1
and assume that
{\mathbb{A}}\sim f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,a+b}
. Then
{\mathbb{X}}\sim f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,b}
is the unique solution to

(4.3)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{A}},\qquad{\mathbb{A}}\mbox{ and }{\mathbb{X}}\mbox{ are freely independent}.

Proof.

Eq. (
4.3
) holds if and only if
S_{\mathbb{X}}=S_{{\mathbb{A}}}S_{{\mathbb{X}}+1}
. We will show that
{\mathbb{X}}
solves (
4.3
) for arbitrary
{\mathbb{A}}
if and only if the following functional equation is satisfied:

(4.4)

\displaystyle(1+S_{\mathbb{X}}(z))S_{{\mathbb{X}}}(z(1+S_{\mathbb{X}}(z)))=S_{\mathbb{A}}(z(1+S_{\mathbb{X}}(z)))S_{\mathbb{X}}(z).

Starting with the identity

\psi_{{\mathbb{X}}+1}\left(\frac{z}{1+z}\right)=z+(1+z)\psi_{\mathbb{X}}(z),

we can rearrange this to obtain

\frac{\chi_{\mathbb{X}}(z)}{1+\chi_{\mathbb{X}}(z)}=\chi_{{\mathbb{X}}+1}\left(z+(1+z)\chi_{\mathbb{X}}(z)\right).

This leads to

\frac{S_{\mathbb{X}}(z)}{1+S_{\mathbb{X}}(z)}=S_{{\mathbb{X}}+1}(z(1+S_{\mathbb{X}}(z))).

The condition
S_{\mathbb{X}}=S_{{\mathbb{A}}}S_{{\mathbb{X}}+1}
then implies the functional equation (
4.4
).

Now, we can verify by a direct computation that if
S_{\mathbb{A}}(z)=\frac{a+b-1-z}{a+z}
, then
S_{\mathbb{X}}(z)=\frac{b-1-z}{a+z}
satisfies (
4.4
). It will follow from other considerations in the paper,
that there are no other solutions to (
4.3
).
∎

4.1.2.
Inverse Marchenko-Pastur distribution

For
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we define the free Generalized Inverse Gaussian (free GIG) distribution by

\displaystyle f{GIG}_{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{\sqrt{(x-a)(b-x)}}{2\pi}\left(\frac{1}{x}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{ab}\,x^{2}}\right)I_{[a,b]}(x)\mathrm{d}x,

where
(a,b)
, with
0<a<b
, is the unique solution to the system of equations:

\displaystyle 1-\lambda+\sqrt{ab}-\frac{a+b}{2ab}=0=1+\lambda+\frac{1}{\sqrt{ab}}-\frac{a+b}{2}.

Typically, the free GIG distribution is defined with three parameters
(\lambda,\alpha,\beta)
, see
[
16
,
19
]
. For our considerations below, we need to restrict ourselves to the case
\alpha=\beta
. We simplify notation by setting
\alpha=\beta=1
.

In
[
32
, Theorem 3.3]
, it was shown that if
{\mathbb{X}}\sim fGIG_{-\lambda}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
has the Marchenko-Pastur distribution
\mu_{\lambda}
, with
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
being freely independent and
\lambda>1
, then

\mathbb{U}=({\mathbb{X}}+{\mathbb{Y}})^{-1}
and
\mathbb{V}={\mathbb{X}}^{-1}-({\mathbb{X}}+{\mathbb{Y}})^{-1}
are also freely independent. Moreover, it follows from consideration in
[
32
, page 383]
that
\mathbb{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{X}}
and
\mathbb{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{Y}}
.

Furthermore, in
[
19
, Theorem 4.1]
, the authors characterized
fGIG_{-\lambda}
through the continued fraction fixed-point equation, i.e.,

{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}({\mathbb{X}}+{\mathbb{Y}})^{-1},

where
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}\sim\mu_{\lambda}
are free and
\lambda>0
.

Using the Hua identity, we have
\mathbb{V}^{-1}={\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{Y}}^{-1}{\mathbb{X}}+{\mathbb{X}}
. Thus, the condition
\mathbb{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{Y}}
implies that

(4.5)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Y}}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{Y}}^{-1}{\mathbb{X}}+{\mathbb{X}},

which is an affine fixed-point equation.
The equality in distribution above was established only under the condition
\lambda>1
, ensuring that the support of
\mu_{\lambda}
is bounded away from
0
. This guarantees that
{\mathbb{Y}}^{-1}
is bounded. However, the inverse of
{\mathbb{Y}}
is also well-defined in the case of
\lambda=1
, when its support is unbounded from above. Specifically, we have

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mu_{{\mathbb{Y}}^{-1}}(\mathrm{d}x)=\mu_{1}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{\sqrt{x-1/4}}{\pi x^{2}}I_{[1/4,+\infty)}(x)\mathrm{d}x,

Since all operations involved in (
4.5
) are continuous in the topology of weak convergence of probability measures, we can take the limit as
\lambda\to 1+
. This allows us to extend the validity of (
4.5
) to the case
\lambda=1
.

4.2.
Existence and uniqueness of the solution

Note that if the distribution of
{\mathbb{X}}
satisfies (
4.1
), then the distribution of
{\mathbb{X}}^{\ast}
also satisfies this equation. Thus, if there exists a unique solution, it must necessarily be self-adjoint.

To simplify the form of the fixed point equation (
4.1
), we consider the polar decomposition of
{\mathbb{A}}
, expressed as
{\mathbb{A}}=U|{\mathbb{A}}|
, where
U
is unitary. Substituting this into our equation yields,

{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{\ast}+{\mathbb{B}}=U(|{\mathbb{A}}|{\mathbb{X}}|{\mathbb{A}}|+U^{\ast}{\mathbb{B}}U)U^{\ast}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}|{\mathbb{A}}|{\mathbb{X}}|{\mathbb{A}}|+U^{\ast}{\mathbb{B}}U,

where
(|{\mathbb{A}}|,U^{\ast}{\mathbb{B}}U)
are *-free from
{\mathbb{X}}
.

Thus, without loss of generality, we may always assume that
{\mathbb{A}}={\mathbb{A}}^{\ast}\geq 0
. From now on, we will consider the following affine fixed-point equation:

(4.7)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{B}},\qquad({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\mbox{ and }{\mathbb{X}}\mbox{ are *-free,}

where
{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
,
{\mathbb{A}}={\mathbb{A}}^{\ast}\geq 0
and
{\mathbb{B}}={\mathbb{B}}^{\ast}\neq 0
.

Theorem 4.2
.

If
\tau({\mathbb{A}})\leq 1
and the series

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}{\mathbb{B}}_{n}{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}

is convergent in distribution, then
\mathbb{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbb{S}

is the unique solution to (
4.7
).

Proof.

It is immediate to check that
\mathbb{S}
solves (
4.7
). Suppose that
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
are two solution to (
4.7
). Take
{\mathbb{X}}_{0}\sim\nu
,
{\mathbb{X}}_{0}^{\prime}\sim\nu^{\prime}
, each free of
({\mathbb{A}}_{n},{\mathbb{B}}_{n})_{n}
, and define two processes: for
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}_{n}={\mathbb{A}}_{n}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}_{n-1}{\mathbb{A}}_{n}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{B}}_{n},\qquad{\mathbb{X}}_{n}^{\prime}={\mathbb{A}}_{n}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}_{n-1}^{\prime}{\mathbb{A}}_{n}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{B}}_{n}.

By (
4.7
), for each
n
, we have
{\mathbb{X}}_{n}\sim\nu
and
{\mathbb{X}}_{n}^{\prime}\sim\nu^{\prime}
. However,

{\mathbb{X}}_{n}-{\mathbb{X}}_{n}^{\prime}={\mathbb{A}}_{n}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}({\mathbb{X}}_{0}-{\mathbb{X}}_{0}^{\prime}){\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n}^{1/2}.

Since under
\tau({\mathbb{A}})\leq 1
,
\Pi_{n}={\mathbb{A}}_{n}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{2}^{1/2}{\mathbb{A}}_{1}{\mathbb{A}}_{2}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n}^{1/2}
converges to zero in distribution (recall results from Section
3.1
),
we see that
{\mathbb{X}}_{n}-{\mathbb{X}}_{n}^{\prime}
also converges to zero in distribution. Therefore, we conclude that
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

The condition
\tau({\mathbb{A}})\leq 1
is not necessary for the existence of a solution to (
4.7
). E.g. if
{\mathbb{A}}c+{\mathbb{B}}=c
for some
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
, then clearly
c
is a solution regardless of any moment condition on
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})
. In fact, in this case, we have

\sum_{n=1}^{k}{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}{\mathbb{B}}_{n}{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}=c(1-\Pi_{k}^{\uparrow}).

Definition 4.4
.

We say that the model (
4.7
) is irreducible if for any
c\in{\mathbb{R}}

{\mathbb{A}}c+{\mathbb{B}}\neq c.

By the representation of the solution in (
4.8
), we deduce the following easy result.

Corollary 4.5
.

(i)

If
{\mathbb{B}}\geq 0
, then
{\mathbb{X}}\geq 0
.

(ii)

If
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}({\mathbb{A}},-{\mathbb{B}})
, then
{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-{\mathbb{X}}
.

Let
\log^{+}(a)=\max\{\log(a),0\}
.

Theorem 4.6
.

The series (
4.8
) is convergent in the following cases:

(i)

almost uniformly if
{\mathbb{A}}=\alpha 1
with
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
{\mathbb{B}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
with
\tau(\log^{+}(|{\mathbb{B}}|))<+\infty
,

(ii)

in norm
\|\cdot\|
if
\tau({\mathbb{A}})<1
and
{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
,

(iii)

in
L^{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}},\tau)
if
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
with
\tau({\mathbb{A}})<1
,
{\mathbb{B}}\geq 0
and
\tau({\mathbb{B}})<+\infty
,

(iv)

bilaterally almost uniformly if
{\mathbb{A}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
with
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
and
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})<+\infty
and
{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
,

(v)

almost uniformly if
{\mathbb{A}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
with
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
and
\mu_{\mathbb{A}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
c>0
, and
{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

Case (i) follows from
[
7
]
.
Case (ii) was proved in Remark
3.2
.
Case (iii) is trivial.
Cases (iv) and (v) follow from Theorem
3.15
. Indeed, if
{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathcal{A}
, then
\|{\mathbb{B}}\|<+\infty
and we can bound
{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{B}}\leq{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+\|{\mathbb{B}}\|\cdot 1_{{\mathcal{A}}}
.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

If
{\mathbb{B}}=c\,{\mathbb{A}}
and
{\mathbb{X}}
solves (
4.7
), then
{\mathbb{Y}}=c^{-1}{\mathbb{X}}+1
solves the modified equation

{\mathbb{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{Y}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+1.

Thus, it follows that a unique solution to (
4.7
) also exists in the case where
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{2}
,
0\leq{\mathbb{B}}\leq c\,{\mathbb{A}}
,
\tau({\mathbb{A}})\leq 1
.

The following result was obtained in the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Lemma 4.8
.

If
{\mathbb{B}}={\mathbb{A}}
and the
S
-transform of
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
satisfies the following functional equation

(1+S_{\mu}(z))S_{\mu}\left(z(1+S_{\mu}(z))\right)=S_{\mathbb{A}}\left(z(1+S_{\mu}(z))\right)S_{\mu}(z),\quad z\in(-\varepsilon,0)

for some
\varepsilon>0
,
then the solution
{\mathbb{X}}
to (
4.7
) has the distribution
\mu
.

4.3.
Moments

As we noted earlier, when
\tau({\mathbb{A}})<1
and
\tau({\mathbb{B}})<+\infty
, the solution to (
4.7
) is given by a series that converges in
L^{1}(\tilde{{\mathcal{A}}},\tau)
. The following lemma shows that, in this regime, the existence of higher moments of
{\mathbb{X}}
is determined by the corresponding moments of
{\mathbb{A}}
and
{\mathbb{B}}
. In contrast, once
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
and
{\mathbb{B}}>0
, the solution
{\mathbb{X}}
fails to possess moments—even if all moments of
{\mathbb{A}}
and
{\mathbb{B}}
exist.

Lemma 4.9
.

Assume
{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}}\geq 0
and
{\mathbb{B}}\neq 0
. The following holds

(i)

If
\tau({\mathbb{A}})<1
,
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{p})<+\infty
and
\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{p})<+\infty
, then
\tau({\mathbb{X}}^{p})<+\infty
.

(ii)

If
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
, then
\tau({\mathbb{X}})=+\infty
.

Proof.

As we observed earlier, the solution to (
4.7
) has a series representation

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}{\mathbb{B}}_{n}{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}.

For any
p\geq 1
, by Minkowski’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbb{X}}\|_{p}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\|{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}{\mathbb{B}}_{n}{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\|_{p}.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
, by (
3.1
),

(4.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}{\mathbb{B}}_{n}{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\|_{p}^{p}&=\tau\left(\left({\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}{\mathbb{B}}_{n}{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\right)^{p}\right)\\
&=\tau\left(\left({\mathbb{B}}_{n}\Pi_{n-1}\right)^{p}\right)=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathrm{NC}}(p)}\kappa_{\pi}({\mathbb{B}}_{n})\tau_{{\mathrm{Kr}}(\pi)}\left(\Pi_{n-1}\right),\end{split}

where we used the fact that
{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}\ldots{\mathbb{A}}_{2}^{1/2}{\mathbb{A}}_{1}{\mathbb{A}}_{2}^{1/2}\ldots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}
has the same distribution as
\Pi_{n-1}
and that it is freely independent from
{\mathbb{B}}_{n}
.
By Theorem
3.6
, for each
k>0
, there exists
C_{k}
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\tau\left(\Pi_{n-1}^{k}\right)\leq C_{k}\,n^{k-1}\tau({\mathbb{A}})^{nk}.

Thus, in the sum on the right-hand side of (
4.9
), the dominating term corresponds to
\pi=1_{p}
. Hence, there exists a constant
D_{p}
such that for all
n
,

\displaystyle\|{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}{\mathbb{B}}_{n}{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}^{1/2}\cdots{\mathbb{A}}_{1}^{1/2}\|_{p}^{p}\leq D_{p}\,n^{p-1}\tau({\mathbb{A}})^{np},

which implies

\displaystyle\|{\mathbb{X}}\|_{p}\leq D_{p}^{1/p}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\tau({\mathbb{A}})^{n}.

The series above is convergent if and only if
\tau({\mathbb{A}})<1
.

The proof of part (ii) is immediate: suppose that
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
. Then, applying
\tau
to both sides of (
4.7
), we obtain

\tau({\mathbb{X}})=\tau({\mathbb{X}})+\tau({\mathbb{B}})
.
Since
\tau({\mathbb{B}})>0
, we conclude that
\tau({\mathbb{X}})
cannot be finite.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

By the main results of this paper (see Section
6
), it follows that
\tau({\mathbb{X}}^{\gamma})<+\infty
for
\gamma\in(0,1/2)
provided
\tau({\mathbb{B}})<+\infty
.

5.
Subordination for
{\mathbb{A}}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}+{\mathbb{B}}

In this section, we present the subordination results relevant to our analysis. Our general setting is that of a
W^{*}
-noncommutative probability space
(\mathcal{A},\tau)
. When all operators
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathbb{A}
, and
\mathbb{B}
belong to
\mathcal{A}
, the subordination formulas hold under the weaker assumption that
\tau
is a faithful state continuous in the wo (weak operator) topology, rather than a trace. However, in the case of unbounded operators, the non-tracial setting introduces significant difficulties because the operators affiliated with an arbitrary von Neumann algebra may not form an algebra. Therefore, we assume that if the variables
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathbb{A}
, and
\mathbb{B}
are unbounded, then
\tau
is a trace.

The results here extend the result of
[
27
]
in several direction. In
[
27
]
, the distribution of
f({\mathbb{A}}){\mathbb{X}}f({\mathbb{A}})+{\mathbb{A}}
was studied under the assumption that all operators were bounded. In this section, we do not require that

\mathbb{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbb{AXA}+\mathbb{B}
so
{\mathbb{X}}
in general is not a perpetuity.
For notational simplicity, we write
\mathbb{AXA}+\mathbb{B}
rather than
{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+\mathbb{B}
as in other sections.

Let
\mathbb{X,A,B}
be self-adjoint operators, affiliated with
\mathcal{A}
, such that
\mathbb{X}
is
*-free from
(\mathbb{A,B})
. Since the cases
\mathbb{X}\in\mathbb{R}\cdot 1_{\mathcal{A}},\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{R}\cdot 1_{\mathcal{A}}
are trivial, we exclude them from the beginning (from now on, we suppress the subscript and denote the unit of our von
Neumann algebra by
1\in\mathcal{A}
). We prove our subordination results in several steps, in contexts with varying degrees of generality.

Lemma 5.1
.

Assume that
\mathbb{A,B,X}
are bounded in
(\mathcal{A},\tau)
, where
\tau
is a normal faithful tracial state, and that
\mathbb{X}
is *-free from
(\mathbb{A,B})

with respect to
\tau
(here we do not need to assume that these variables are selfadjoint). Assume also that
\mathbb{A}
is invertible in
\mathcal{A}
. Then there exists an
analytic function
f
defined on a neighborhood of infinity such that

\tau\left(\left(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}\mathbb{X}\mathbb{A}\right)^{-1}\right)=\tau\left(\left(\mathbb{A}f(z)\mathbb{A}+z-\mathbb{B}\right)^{-1}\right).

If in addition
\mathbb{A,B,X}
are selfadjoint, then
f
extends to an analytic self-map of
\mathbb{C}^{+}
