\displaystyle\lim_{v\to+\infty}\tau\left(\mathbb{A}\left(\frac{\Re z-\mathbb{B}}{iv}+\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to+\infty}\tau\left(\mathbb{A}\left(\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\mbox{}\times\left(\left(\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\Re z-\mathbb{B}}{iv}\left(\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}+1\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{A}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to+\infty}\!\tau\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\left(\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\!\!\left(\!\left(\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\!\frac{\Re z-\mathbb{B}}{iv}\left(\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\!+1\right)^{\!-1}\right)\!.

It is well-known (and easy to check, for instance via Borel functional calculus) that

\left\|\mathbb{A}^{2}\left(\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\right\|\leq 1
and

\mathbb{A}^{2}\left(\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\to 1-P_{\ker(\mathbb{A})}
as

v\to+\infty
, in the so topology, where
P_{\ker(\mathbb{A})}
is the orthogonal projection onto
the kernel of
\mathbb{A}
. Similarly,
\|(\Im z+v\mathbb{A}^{2})^{\frac{-1}{2}}\|\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Im z}}
and

(\Im z+v\mathbb{A}^{2})^{\frac{-1}{2}}\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Im z}}P_{\ker(\mathbb{A})}
as
v\to+\infty
in the so topology.
Then
\left(\!\left(\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\!\frac{\Re z-\mathbb{B}}{iv}\left(\frac{\Im z}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\!+1\right)^{\!-1}\to i\big(P_{\ker(\mathbb{A})}\frac{\Re z-\mathbb{B}}{\Im z}P_{\ker(\mathbb{A})}+i\big)^{-1}
as
v\to+\infty
(easily seen to
happen in the wo topology). Both families stay bounded in norm, uniformly in
v
(both by one).
Thus, their product converges as well in the wo topology, and to the product of the limits. It follows that

(5.49)

\lim_{v\to+\infty}iv\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+iv\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)=1-\tau(P_{\ker(\mathbb{A})}).

It follows that
\Im\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+w_{1}\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}>\frac{\Im w_{1}}{1-\tau(P_{\ker(\mathbb{A})})}\geq\Im w_{1}
, for all
w_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
(obviously, we do
use the fact that
\mathbb{A}\neq 0
). This allows us to conclude that
\mathscr{F}_{z}
is well-defined, sending

\mathbb{C}^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{+}
into itself.

There are several ways now to argue that
\mathscr{F}_{z}
has a unique attracting fixed point inside

\mathbb{C}^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{+}
(the analyticity of the dependence of this attracting fixed point on
z
is
a trivial normal families argument, once its existence and uniqueness for an arbitrary
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
has been established).
Consider the function

g_{z}\colon w\mapsto\frac{1}{\tau\left(\left(\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}\left(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-w-\mathbb{X}\right)^{-1}\right)}-\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}+w,

for fixed
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}.
We have seen that it is well-defined, and (since
\mathbb{A,X}
are not multiples of the identity) that
it maps
\mathbb{C}^{+}
into itself. Moreover, since
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, it extends continuously (in fact smoothly) to
\mathbb{R}
,
and sends
\mathbb{C}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}
into
\mathbb{C}^{+}
. This, in particular, guarantees that its Denjoy-Wolff point
(exists and) is not in
\mathbb{R}
. We claim it cannot be
\infty
either. To be clear, infinity being the Denjoy-Wolff
point of
g_{z}
would mean
that
\lim_{v\to+\infty}g_{z}(iv)=\infty
and
\lim_{v\to+\infty}\frac{g_{z}(iv)}{iv}
exists and belongs to
[1,+\infty)
.
That is a straightforward, but long, argument: if the nontangential limit at infinity of this function either does not exist or is
finite (i.e. if
\lim_{v\to+\infty}g_{z}(iv)\neq\infty
), then we’re done. As already
seen, if
\ker\mathbb{A}
is nontrivial, then
\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}\left(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-w
tends to infinity as
w\to+\infty

nontangentially and its Julia-Carathéodory derivative is
\frac{\tau(P_{\ker(\mathbb{A})})}{1-\tau(P_{\ker(\mathbb{A})})}
, so that the Julia-Carathéodory derivative
of
g_{z}
at infinity is zero, which shows that infinity is not attracting for it. If

w\mapsto\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}\left(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-w
does not have a nontangential limit at
infinity or it has a nontangential limit at infinity which is in
\mathbb{C}^{+}
, then the nontangential limit at infinity of
g_{z}
either is in
\mathbb{C}^{+}

or does not exist. In both cases,
g_{z}(w)
does not have infinity as an attracting fixed point. Thus, the only possibility that remains to be discarded is that

w\mapsto\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}\left(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-w
has a finite real nontangential limit at infinity,

\lim_{v\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}\left(z-\mathbb{B}+iv\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-iv=\ell\in\mathbb{R},\text{ that }\ \sphericalangle\lim_{u\to\ell}\tau\left((\mathbb{X}-u)^{-1}\right)=0,

and

\lim_{v\to+\infty}\frac{1}{iv\tau\left(\left(\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}\left(z-\mathbb{B}+iv\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-iv-\!\mathbb{X}\right)^{-1}\right)}-\frac{\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+iv\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-iv}{iv}\in[1,+\infty).

Remarkably, only the first condition is needed in order to obtain a contradiction. Let us first see what one can conclude from
\lim_{v\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}\left(z-\mathbb{B}+iv\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-iv=\ell
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\ni\ell

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}\left(z-\mathbb{B}+iv\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-iv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to+\infty}\frac{\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B})\left(z-\mathbb{B}+iv\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\right)}{\tau\left(\left(z-\mathbb{B}+iv\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to+\infty}\frac{\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B})\left(\frac{z-\mathbb{B}}{iv}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\right)}{\tau\left(\left(\frac{z-\mathbb{B}}{iv}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)}.

The limit of the denominator exists and equals one (recall that we have already discarded the case
\ker(\mathbb{A})\neq\{0\}
).
Accounting for traciality, one has

\displaystyle\Im\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B})\left(\frac{z-\mathbb{B}}{iv}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\right)=\Im\tau\left((x-\mathbb{B}+iy)\left(\frac{y}{v}+\mathbb{A}^{2}-i\frac{x-\mathbb{B}}{v}\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Im\tau\!\left(\!(x\!-\!\mathbb{B}+iy)\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!\right)^{\!-\frac{1}{2}}\!\!\left[1\!-\!i\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{\!-\frac{1}{2}}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!\right)^{\!-\frac{1}{2}}\right]^{-1}\!\!\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{\!-\frac{1}{2}}\!\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Im\tau\left(\!(x\!-\!\mathbb{B}+iy)\left[1\!+\!i\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\right]\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!+\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\right]^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\left(\left[(x-\mathbb{B})\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}+y\right]\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!+\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\right]^{-1}\right).

We have used
\left(\mathbb{A}^{2}+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{-1}\frac{x-\mathbb{B}}{v}\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}+\frac{y}{v}+\frac{x-\mathbb{B}}{v}\left(\mathbb{A}^{2}+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{-1}\frac{x-\mathbb{B}}{v}\right]^{-1}
being selfadjoint, and the fact that
\Im\tau((a+ib)(c+id))=\tau(ad+bc)
for
a,b,c,d
selfadjoint and
\tau
tracial.
Clearly
(x-\mathbb{B})\left(\mathbb{A}^{2}+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{-1}\frac{x-\mathbb{B}}{v}\geq 0
,
y>0
, so the only possibility for
the above quantity to go to zero is if

(5.50)

\lim_{v\to+\infty}\tau\left(\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!+\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\right]^{-1}\right)=0,

\lim_{v\to+\infty}\tau\left((x-\mathbb{B})\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!+\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\right]^{-1}\right)=0.

Clearly,
\frac{y}{v}\!+\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}=\frac{y}{v}\!+\!(x\!-\!\mathbb{B})\!\left(\!(v\mathbb{A})^{2}\!+vy\right)^{\!-1}\!(x\!-\!\mathbb{B})
, and

v<v^{\prime}\iff(v\mathbb{A})^{2}+vy<(v^{\prime}\mathbb{A})^{2}+v^{\prime}y\iff\left(\!(v\mathbb{A})^{2}\!+vy\right)^{\!-1}>\left(\!(v^{\prime}\mathbb{A})^{2}\!+v^{\prime}y\right)^{\!-1}\implies(x-\mathbb{B})\left(\!(v\mathbb{A})^{2}\!+vy\right)^{\!-1}(x-\mathbb{B})\geq(x-\mathbb{B})\left(\!(v^{\prime}\mathbb{A})^{2}\!+v^{\prime}y\right)^{\!-1}(x-\mathbb{B})\implies\frac{x-\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x-\mathbb{B}}{v}+\frac{y}{v}>\frac{y}{v^{\prime}}+\frac{x-\mathbb{B}}{v^{\prime}}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}+\frac{y}{v^{\prime}}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x-\mathbb{B}}{v^{\prime}}\iff\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}+\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}>\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v^{\prime}}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v^{\prime}}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v^{\prime}}+\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v^{\prime}}\iff\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!+\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\right]^{-1}\!\!<\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v^{\prime}}\!+\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v^{\prime}}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v^{\prime}}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v^{\prime}}\!\right]^{-1}
.
Thus,
(0,+\infty)\ni v\mapsto\tau\left(\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\!+\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\left(\!\mathbb{A}^{2}\!+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x\!-\!\mathbb{B}}{v}\!\right]^{-1}\right)
is increasing as a function from
(0,+\infty)
to itself. But that excludes the possibility
that the limit (
5.50
) is zero, since
\tau
is faithful and for
v,y>0
fixed, the operator
\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}+\frac{y}{v}+\frac{x-\mathbb{B}}{v}\left(\mathbb{A}^{2}+\frac{y}{v}\right)^{\!-1}\!\frac{x-\mathbb{B}}{v}\right]^{-1}
is bounded and nonzero.
This forces the function

(5.51)

g_{z}\colon w\mapsto\frac{1}{\tau\left(\left(\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}\left(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-w-\mathbb{X}\right)^{-1}\right)}-\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}+w,

(
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
fixed) to have a unique attracting fixed point (the Denjoy-Wolff point) inside

\mathbb{C}^{+}
. This fixed point is simply
f(z)
, and
\mathsf{f}(z)=\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-f(z)
.
A direct verification shows that
\mathscr{F}_{z}(f(z),{\sf f}(z))=(f(z),{\sf f}(z))
. The analytic dependence
on
z
of
f(z)
has been explained above via a normal families argument, but we should note that it
could also be obtained via the implicit function theorem because the derivative of a function at its
Denjoy-Wolff point in
\mathbb{C}^{+}
is necessarily of absolute value strictly less than one.
∎

Let us state the main result (and the reason for) this section.

Corollary 5.8
.

Assume that
(\mathcal{A},\tau)
is a tracial
W^{*}
-noncommutative probability space,

\mathbb{X,A,B}
are selfadjoint random variables (possibly unbounded) affiliated with
\mathcal{A}
,
and
\mathbb{X}
is *-free from
(\mathbb{A,B})
with respect to
\tau
. Assume in addition that
neither of
\mathbb{A,X}
is a scalar multiple of the unit
1\in\mathcal{A}
.
Then there exist two analytic self-maps
\mathsf{f},f\colon\mathbb{C}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}^{+}
of the
complex upper half-plane such that

(5.52)

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau((\mathsf{f}(z)-\mathbb{X})^{-1})}-\mathsf{f}(z),

(5.53)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-f(z),

(5.54)

\displaystyle\tau\left((\mathsf{f}(z)-\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{AXA})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)

(5.55)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right),

(5.56)

\displaystyle\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{AXA})^{-1}\right),

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
. Moreover, if
\tau(\mathbb{A}^{2})<+\infty
, then

\lim_{v\to+\infty}\frac{\mathsf{f}(iv)}{iv}=\frac{1}{\tau(\mathbb{A}^{2})},\ \lim_{v\to+\infty}\frac{f(iv)}{iv}=0,\quad\lim_{v\to+\infty}f(iv)=\lim_{y\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\tau\left((iy-\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)}-iy=-\tau(\mathbb{X}).

The last equality should be understood in the following sense: if

\int_{\mathbb{R}}t\,{\rm d}\mu_{\mathbb{X}}(t)=m\in\mathbb{R}
, then
\lim_{v\to+\infty}f(iv)=-m
. If
the integral is not well-defined, then the limit does not exist in
\mathbb{R}
(however, it might
exist in
\mathbb{C}^{+}
, and then the last equality fails).

Proof.

The existence of the pair
(f,\mathsf{f})
satisfying (
5.52
) and (
5.53
) is Theorem
5.7
. We have proved that

\mathsf{f}
satisfies (
5.54
) in Proposition
5.6
under the supplementary assumption that
\tau(\mathbb{A}^{2}+\mathbb{X}^{2})<+\infty
. Observe that we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathbb{A}\geq 0
, since otherwise
(thanks to the traciality of
\tau)
none of the above expressions changes
4
4

4

This might require a proof only for (
5.56
):
if
\mathbb{A}=V\sqrt{\mathbb{A}^{2}}
is the polar decomposition, then
V\sqrt{\mathbb{A}^{2}}=\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}^{*}=\sqrt{\mathbb{A}^{2}}V^{*}
(with
V
unitary - we are in a tracial
W^{*}
-probability space), so
\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{AXA})^{-1}\right)=\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B}+V\sqrt{\mathbb{A}^{2}}\mathbb{X}\sqrt{\mathbb{A}^{2}}V^{*})^{-1}\right)=\tau\left((z-V^{*}\mathbb{B}V+\sqrt{\mathbb{A}^{2}}\mathbb{X}\sqrt{\mathbb{A}^{2}})^{-1}\right)
. Replacing
\mathbb{B}

with
V^{*}\mathbb{B}V
in the left hand side of (
5.56
) changes nothing, since
\mathbb{A}^{2}=V\mathbb{A}^{2}V^{*}
.
when
replacing
\mathbb{A}
with
\sqrt{\mathbb{A}^{2}}
. Thus, from now on, we assume
\mathbb{A}\geq 0
.
From Proposition
5.4
, by multiplying left and right in (
5.7
) by
\mathbb{A}
, we also
have that (
5.55
), (
5.56
) hold for bounded
\mathbb{A}
and arbitrary selfadjoints
\mathbb{X,B}
. This
guarantees the existence of an
f
under these hypotheses. We
define

\mathsf{f}
by (
5.53
) and obtain (
5.52
)
(thanks to an application of Theorem
5.7
). Plugging (
5.55
) into (
5.53
) proves the corollary also under the hypotheses of
bounded
\mathbb{A}
and arbitrary
\mathbb{B,X}
.

We extend the corollary to arbitrary
\mathbb{A}\geq 0
as well. To clarify, all that follows below is only necessary for
proving the parts of the corollary involving
\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{AXA}
, Equations (
5.52
), (
5.53
), and
(
5.55
) follow directly from Theorem
5.7
. As before, we approximate
\mathbb{A}
with

\mathbb{A}_{n}=\Theta_{n}(\mathbb{A})
, where
\Theta_{n}(t)=t
if
0\leq t\leq n
and
\Theta_{n}(t)=n
if
t>n

(we do not need
\chi_{n}
from the proof of Proposition
5.4
because we do not care to have
\{\mathbb{A}_{n}\}^{\prime\prime}=\{\mathbb{A}\}^{\prime\prime}
here). We apply the approximation in (
5.54
) and (
5.55
) first.
A bound of the form
\big\|\mathbb{A}_{n}(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n}\mathbb{XA}_{n})^{-1}\mathbb{A}_{n}\big\|\leq\frac{n^{2}}{|\Im z|}
is obvious. As noted above, we have proved that
f_{n}
exists for
\mathbb{A}_{n}
. Since

w\mapsto(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}
is bounded by
1/\Im z
uniformly in
n
and

\displaystyle(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{m}^{2})^{-1}-(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}_{n}\big)^{-1}\big(\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}-\mathbb{A}_{m}^{2})\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{m}^{2}\big)^{-1},

for all
m,n
, we easily obtain that

\displaystyle 0\leq\big(\overline{z}-\mathbb{B}+\overline{w}\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}(\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}-\mathbb{A}^{2})\big|z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}_{n}\big|^{-2}(\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}-\mathbb{A}^{2})\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Im z|^{2}}\big(\overline{z}-\mathbb{B}+\overline{w}\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}(\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}-\mathbb{A}^{2})^{2}\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Im z|^{2}}\big(\overline{z}-\mathbb{B}+\overline{w}\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}q_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}-\mathbb{A}^{2})^{2}q_{n}\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Im z|^{2}}\big(\overline{z}-\mathbb{B}+\overline{w}\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}q_{n}\mathbb{A}^{4}q_{n}\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1},

(recall the previously introduced
q_{n}=\bm{1}_{[n,+\infty)}(\mathbb{A})
, so that
q_{n}\searrow 0
in the so topology) and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Im z|^{2}}\left\langle\big(\overline{z}-\mathbb{B}+\overline{w}\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}q_{n}\mathbb{A}^{4}q_{n}\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}\xi,\xi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\!=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Im z|^{2}}\left\langle q_{n}\mathbb{A}^{2}q_{n}\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}\xi,q_{n}\mathbb{A}^{2}q_{n}\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}\xi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\!=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Im z|^{2}}\left\langle q_{n}\mathbb{A}^{2}\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}\xi,q_{n}\mathbb{A}^{2}\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}\xi\right\rangle\to 0\text{ as }n\to+\infty,

since
q_{n}\to 0
in the so topology and
\mathbb{A}^{2}\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}
is a bounded operator
(recall that
\sigma(UV)\cup\{0\}=\sigma(VU)\cup\{0\}
- one applies this to
U=\mathbb{A},V=\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B})^{-1}

in
\mathbb{A}^{2}(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}=\frac{1}{w}\big[1-(z-\mathbb{B})(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\big]=\frac{1}{w}-\frac{1}{w}\big(1+w\mathbb{A}^{2}(z-\mathbb{B})^{-1}\big)^{-1}=\frac{1}{w}-\frac{1}{w^{2}}\big(w^{-1}+\mathbb{A}^{2}(z-\mathbb{B})^{-1}\big)^{-1}
) and independent of
n
. This shows that
\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}_{n}\big)^{-1}\to\big(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}
in the so topology. On the other hand, functions
f_{n}
provided by
Proposition
5.4
, coinciding with those
f_{n}
’s provided by Theorem
5.7
, form a normal family. Picking a convergent
subsequence
\{f_{n_{k}}\}_{k}
makes
\big(z-\mathbb{B}+f_{n_{k}}(z)\mathbb{A}^{2}_{n_{k}}\big)^{-1}\to\big(z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}
in the so topology. By taking limits in (
5.56
) (which, as mentioned above, is
known for
\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}
),

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right)=\tau\big((z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\big).

This does not tell us yet that
f
coincides with the function
f
provided by Theorem
5.7
for the given
\mathbb{A,B,X}
.
To establish the uniqueness of the limit (i.e. the independence of the subsequence considered), and the fact that this limit
coincides with the
f
associated with
\mathbb{A,B,X}
from Theorem
5.7
, let us estimate the difference

\displaystyle\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)-\tau\left(\mathbb{A}_{n}(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}^{2}-(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}^{2}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\left((z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\!\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}-\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}\right]+\!\left[(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\!-(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}\right]\mathbb{A}^{2}_{n}\right).

As we have already noted,
\mathbb{A}^{2}\geq\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}
and
\mathbb{A}^{2}-\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}=q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})=(\mathbb{A}^{2}-\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})q_{n}
, so that

(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}-\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}\right]^{2}(\overline{z}-\mathbb{B}+\overline{w}\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\leq 4(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}^{2}q_{n}\mathbb{A}^{2}(\overline{z}-\mathbb{B}+\overline{w}\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}
. Precisely
as above,
\|q_{n}\mathbb{A}^{2}(\overline{z}-\mathbb{B}+\overline{w}\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\xi\|_{2}\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
for any
\xi\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\tau).
Thus,
\left[\mathbb{A}^{2}-\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}\right]\!(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\!\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
in
the so topology. Since
(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\!-(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}\to 0
in the so topology,

\tau\left(\left[(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\!-(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}\right]\mathbb{A}^{2}_{n}\right)
also converges to zero.

Indeed,
(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\!-(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}=w(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}(\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}-\mathbb{A}^{2})(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}=-w(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}
. Observe also that if
\mathbb{A}
(and hence
\mathbb{A}_{n}
) is
invertible, then
0\leq q_{n}\mathbb{A}^{-j}=\mathbb{A}^{-j}q_{n}\leq\frac{q_{n}}{n^{j}}
,
q_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{-j}=\mathbb{A}_{n}^{-j}q_{n}=\frac{q_{n}}{n^{j}}
. This tells us that one can make sense of
q_{n}\mathbb{A}^{-j}=\mathbb{A}^{-j}q_{n}
for non-invertible
\mathbb{A}

as well (with the same estimate), and that
q_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{-j}=\mathbb{A}_{n}^{-j}q_{n}=\frac{q_{n}}{n^{j}}
holds as well, regardless of the invertibility of
\mathbb{A}
. Just in this paragraph, for saving space, let

R=(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1},R_{n}=(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}
. One writes
[\mathbb{A}_{n}(R-R_{n})\mathbb{A}_{n}]^{*}[\mathbb{A}_{n}(R-R_{n})\mathbb{A}_{n}]=|w|^{2}[\mathbb{A}_{n}Rq_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}R_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n}]^{*}[\mathbb{A}_{n}Rq_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}R_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n}]=|w|^{2}\mathbb{A}_{n}R^{*}_{n}q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}R^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}Rq_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n}
. Under the trace, this is the same as

|w|^{2}\mathbb{A}_{n}Rq_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}R_{n}^{*}q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}
, and

|w|^{2}\mathbb{A}_{n}Rq_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}R_{n}^{*}q_{n}\allowbreak(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}\!=|w|^{2}\!\mathbb{A}_{n}Rq_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{-1}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})\mathbb{A}_{n}R_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}R_{n}^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})\mathbb{A}_{n}^{-1}q_{n}R^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}\!={|w|^{2}}\!\mathbb{A}_{n}Rq_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})\mathbb{A}_{n}^{-1}(\mathbb{A}_{n}R_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n})(\mathbb{A}_{n}R_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n})^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{-1}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}R^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}\leq|w|^{2}\left|\frac{1}{|w|}+\frac{1}{|\Im w|}\right|^{2}\!\mathbb{A}_{n}R(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{-2}q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}=\left|1+\frac{|w|}{|\Im w|}\right|^{2}\!\frac{\mathbb{A}_{n}R(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}}{n^{2}}
. Again, under the trace
this is the same as
\left|1+\frac{|w|}{|\Im w|}\right|^{2}\!\frac{q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}R(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}}{n^{2}}
, and

\displaystyle\left|1+\frac{|w|}{|\Im w|}\right|^{2}\!\frac{q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}R(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}}{n^{2}}\!

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|1+\frac{|w|}{|\Im w|}\right|^{2}\!\!q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R^{*}R(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|1+\frac{|w|}{|\Im w|}\right|^{2}\!\|R(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}\|^{2}q_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|1+\frac{|w|}{|\Im w|}\right|^{2}\!\|R(\mathbb{A}^{2}\!-n^{2})q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}\!-n^{2})R^{*}\|q_{n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\left|1+\frac{|w|}{|\Im w|}\right|^{2}\!\|R\mathbb{A}^{2}\|^{2}q_{n},

with the (very rough) majorization
0\leq(\mathbb{A}^{2}\!-n^{2})q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}\!-n^{2})\leq 4\mathbb{A}^{4}
, easily obtainable via
continuous functional calculus. We have used in the above the fact that
\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}=w^{-1}-w^{-2}\big(w^{-1}+\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\big)^{-1}
for any selfadjoints
\mathbb{A,B}
(bounded or not),
and
\big\|\big(w^{-1}+\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\big)^{-1}\big\|\leq|\Im(w^{-1})|=\frac{|\Im w|}{|w|^{2}}
.
Similarly,
R\mathbb{A}^{2}=(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}^{2}=\frac{1}{w}[1-(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}(z-\mathbb{B})]=\frac{1}{w}-\frac{1}{w^{2}}\big(\frac{1}{w}+(z-\mathbb{B})^{-1}\mathbb{A}^{2}\big)^{-1}.
Applying the known fact
that
\sigma(UV)\cup\{0\}=\sigma(VU)\cup\{0\}
to
U=(z-\mathbb{B})\mathbb{A},V=\mathbb{A}
, guarantees that

\|R\mathbb{A}^{2}\|<+\infty
, uniformly in
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+},

w
running in compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}^{+}
. Finally, we
have also used the definition of
\mathbb{A}_{n},
which tells us that
0\leq\mathbb{A}_{n}\leq\|\mathbb{A}_{n}\|\leq n
. All this tells us that

\displaystyle\tau\!\left(\mathbb{A}_{n}\!\left[(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\!-(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}\right]^{*}\!\!\mathbb{A}^{2}_{n}\!\left[(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\!-(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}\right]\!\mathbb{A}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=

\displaystyle\tau\left([\mathbb{A}_{n}(R-R_{n})\mathbb{A}_{n}]^{*}[\mathbb{A}_{n}(R-R_{n})\mathbb{A}_{n}]\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq

\displaystyle\left|1+\frac{|w|}{|\Im w|}\right|^{2}\tau\left(\!\frac{\mathbb{A}_{n}R(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}}{n^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=

\displaystyle\left|1+\frac{|w|}{|\Im w|}\right|^{2}\tau\left(\!\frac{q_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})R^{*}\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2}R(\mathbb{A}^{2}-n^{2})q_{n}}{n^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq

\displaystyle 4\left|1+\frac{|w|}{|\Im w|}\right|^{2}\!\|R\mathbb{A}^{2}\|^{2}\tau(q_{n})\to 0\text{ as }n\to+\infty.\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad

By the Cauchy-Schwarz-Buniakowski inequality for states, we conclude that

\tau([(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\!-(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}]\mathbb{A}^{2}_{n})=\tau\left(\mathbb{A}_{n}\left[(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\!-(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}\right]\mathbb{A}_{n}\right)\to 0

as
n\to+\infty
, at a rate proportional to
\tau(q_{n})
, uniformly in
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
and
w
in compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}^{+}
.
This completes the argument that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\tau\left(\mathbb{A}_{n}(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}_{n}\right)=\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right).

Thus, if
g_{z,n}
is obtained from the function
g_{z}
in the proof of Theorem
5.7
(see
(
5.51
)) by replacing
\mathbb{A}
with
\mathbb{A}_{n}
, then we have proved that

g_{z,n}\to g_{z}
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}^{+}
.
As shown by Heins in
[
20
]
, this implies that the Denjoy-Wolff points
f_{n}(z)
of
g_{z,n}

converge to the Denjoy-Wolff point
f(z)
of
g_{z}
. Thanks to (
5.52
), (
5.53
) (which hold for
arbitrary
\mathbb{A,B,X}
), this shows also that
\mathsf{f}_{n}(z)\to\mathsf{f}(z)
, meaning that the
subsequential limit described in the first part of the proof is independent of the specific subsequence
we choose, and nontrivial (that is,
f(\mathbb{C}^{+})\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{+}
is not a constant). Meaning, we have proved that

\tau\big((z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n}\mathbb{XA}_{n})^{-1}\big)=\tau\big((z-\mathbb{B}+f_{n}(z)\mathbb{A}_{n}^{2})^{-1}\big)\to\tau\big((z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\big)
, as claimed.
All we still need to argue is that this limit coincides with
\tau\big((z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}\mathbb{XA})^{-1}\big)
. That is rather straightforward: for instance, the boundedness of
\|(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n}\mathbb{XA}_{n})^{-1}\|
(by
\epsilon^{-1}
) on
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}+i\epsilon
forces the existence of a wo-convergent subsequence: for each
z
,

\lim_{k\to+\infty}(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}=V(z)
. Pick
5
5

5

All this
can be found in the literature, but for the sake of completeness, we give an argument here.
a countable dense subset
\{z_{m}\colon m\in\mathbb{N}\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{+}
and, via a diagonal extraction process, find a possibly further subsequence such that the wo limit

\lim_{k\to+\infty}(z_{m}-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}=V(z_{m})
exists for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Any such wo limit depends wo-analytically on
z
: for any wo-continuous positive linear functional
\varphi
on
\mathcal{A}
,
the map
z\mapsto\varphi\left((z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right)
sends
\mathbb{C}^{+}
into
\mathbb{C}^{-}
,
and
\lim_{k\to+\infty}\varphi\left((z_{m}-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right)=\varphi(V(z_{m}))
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus, thanks to Montel’s theorem, there exists a unique analytic map
\mathsf{g}_{\varphi}(z)
on
\mathbb{C}^{+}
with values in

\mathbb{C}^{-}
such that
\mathsf{g}_{\varphi}(z_{m})=\varphi(V(z_{m}))
on the dense subset
\{z_{m}\colon m\in\mathbb{N}\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{+}
.
The linearity of the correspondence
\varphi\mapsto\mathsf{g}_{\varphi}
is obvious, so, by the completeness of a von Neumann algebra in
the wo topology,
z_{m}\mapsto V(z_{m})
extends to all of
\mathbb{C}^{+}
by wo-continuity, with
\Im V(z)\leq 0,z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
. We claim that
V(z)
is the resolvent of
\mathbb{AXA}+\mathbb{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
.
Indeed, note that
\lim_{k\to+\infty}(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}=V(z)
in the wo topology: if
\xi
is given,
then

\displaystyle\left\langle\left[(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}-V(z)\right]\xi,\xi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\left[(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}-(z_{j}-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right]\xi,\xi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\mbox{}+\left\langle\left[(z_{j}-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}-V(z_{j})\right]\xi,\xi\right\rangle+\langle[V(z_{j})-V(z)]\xi,\xi\rangle.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, the continuity of
V
in the weak operator topology (shown above) guarantees that there exits a
\delta_{1}>0

(depending on
\varepsilon,z,
and
\xi
) such that
|\langle[V(z_{j})-V(z)]\xi,\xi\rangle|<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
for
all
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
|z-z_{j}|<\delta_{1}
. For the resolvent, one has

\|(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}-(z_{j}-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\|=|z_{j}-z|\|(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}(z_{j}-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\|\leq\frac{|z_{j}-z|}{\Im z\Im z_{j}}
, providing an easy
\delta_{2}>0
, this one only depending on
\Im z
and
\varepsilon
, not on
\xi
.
Fix a
z_{j}
that works for these two terms. Since
(z_{j}-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\to V(z_{j})

as
k\to+\infty
, there exists a
k_{\varepsilon,j,\xi}\in\mathbb{N}
depending on all of
{\varepsilon,j,\xi}
, such that
for all
k\geq k_{\varepsilon,j,\xi}
, one has
\left|\left\langle\left[(z_{j}-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}-V(z_{j})\right]\xi,\xi\right\rangle\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
. Thus, for all
k\geq k_{\varepsilon,j,\xi}
, one has
\left|\left\langle\left[(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}-V(z)\right]\xi,\xi\right\rangle\right|<\varepsilon
. Having established this limit to hold, let us consider the product
(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})V(z)
.
By its definition, for any
\xi
in the domain of
\mathbb{A}
, one has
\lim_{k\to+\infty}\|(\mathbb{A}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}})\xi\|_{2}=\lim_{k\to+\infty}\|q_{n_{k}}(\mathbb{A}-{n_{k}})q_{n_{k}}\xi\|_{2}=0.
Since all of
\mathbb{A,X,B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
,
there exists a dense subspace of the Hilbert space on which
\mathcal{A}
acts such that all of

\mathbb{A,X,B,AX,XA,AXA,AXA+B}
have it as a common domain. For a
\xi
in this dense subspace, one has
that
\mathbb{XA}\xi
belongs to the domain of
\mathbb{A}
, so that

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\|(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\mathbb{XA}\xi\|_{2}=0
, and

\|\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{X}(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi\|_{2}^{2}=\langle\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{X}(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi,\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{X}(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi\rangle=\langle\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}^{2}[\mathbb{X}(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi],[\mathbb{X}(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi]\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{A}^{2}[\mathbb{X}(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi],[\mathbb{X}(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi]\rangle=\|(\mathbb{AX})(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi\|_{2}^{2}
. Since
(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi
belongs to the domain of

\mathbb{AX}
for all
k
and
\lim_{k\to+\infty}\|(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi\|_{2}=0
, we also have

0=\lim_{k\to+\infty}\|(\mathbb{AX})(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi\|_{2}\geq\lim_{k\to+\infty}\|(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{X})(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi\|_{2}\geq 0
. Thus,

0\leq\lim_{k\to+\infty}\|(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{AXA})\xi\|_{2}\leq\lim_{k\to+\infty}\|(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{X})(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\xi\|_{2}+\|(\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{A})\mathbb{XA}\xi\|_{2}=0.

We have

\displaystyle\left\langle(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})V(z)\xi,\xi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})\left[V(z)-(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right]\xi,\xi\right\rangle+\langle\xi,\xi\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\left[V(z)-(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right]\xi,(z-\mathbb{B})\xi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\mbox{}-\left\langle\left[V(z)-(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right]\xi,\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}}\xi\right\rangle+\langle\xi,\xi\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\left[V(z)-(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right]\xi,(z-\mathbb{B})\xi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\mbox{}-\left\langle\left[V(z)-(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right]\xi,[\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{AXA}]\xi\right\rangle

\displaystyle\mbox{}-\left\langle\left[V(z)-(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right]\xi,\mathbb{AXA}\xi\right\rangle+\langle\xi,\xi\rangle.

Given that
\xi
is in the domain of
\mathbb{B}
, the weak operator convergence of
(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}
to
V(z)
yields
\lim_{k\to+\infty}\left\langle\left[V(z)-(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right]\xi,(z-\mathbb{B})\xi\right\rangle=0
.
By the same argument,
\lim_{k\to+\infty}\left\langle\left[V(z)-(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right]\xi,\mathbb{AXA}\xi\right\rangle=0
as well.
Since
\|V(z)\|\leq\frac{1}{\Im z},\|(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\|\leq\frac{1}{\Im z}
, one has by the Schwartz-Cauchy-Buniakowski inequality

0\leq|\left\langle\left[V(z)-(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})^{-1}\right]\xi,[\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{AXA}]\xi\right\rangle|\leq\frac{2}{\Im z}\|\xi\|_{2}\|[\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}}-\mathbb{AXA}]\xi\|_{2}\to 0
as
k\to+\infty
, as proven above.
Thus,

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\left\langle(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})V(z)\xi,\xi\right\rangle=\langle\xi,\xi\rangle.

This tells us that
(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})V(z)\to 1
in the wo topology.
Since
(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}_{n_{k}}\mathbb{XA}_{n_{k}})\to(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}\mathbb{XA})

in the so topology, one has
(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}\mathbb{XA})V(z)=1
. The proof that
V(z)(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}\mathbb{XA})\xi=\xi
for all
\xi
in the domain of
\mathbb{AXA}+\mathbb{B}

is straightforward. We conclude that
V(z)=(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{A}\mathbb{XA})^{-1}
.

This concludes the proof of (
5.56
), and hence of (
5.54
)–(
5.55
) as well, and hence of the existence part of the corollary.

It remains to study the asymptotics of
f,\mathsf{f}
at infinity under the assumption that
\tau(\mathbb{A}^{2})<+\infty
.
Proposition
5.6
shows that
\lim_{y\to+\infty}\frac{\mathsf{f}(iy)}{iy}=\frac{1}{\tau(\mathbb{A}^{2})}
under the supplementary assumption that

\tau(\mathbb{X}^{2})<+\infty
, an assumption that we shall easily remove next. Indeed, recall
that the hypothesys
\mathbb{X}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\tau)
in Proposition
5.6
is used only
in the proof of the
existence
of
\mathsf{f}
, and the existence of
f
has now been
established in Theorem
5.7
without making this assumption. Thus, at this moment
we can argue directly from (
5.54
) (by multiplying with
z
on both sides and letting

z
go to infinity nontangentially) that
\mathsf{f}(iv)/iv\to\tau(\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}
. We can also
argue directly from Theorem
5.7
: since we know, thanks to the Julia-Carathéodory Theorem that

c:=\lim_{v\to+\infty}f(iv)/iv,k:=\lim_{v\to+\infty}\mathsf{f}(iv)/iv
exist and belong to
[0,+\infty)
,
from the functional equations in Theorem
5.7
we obtain by multiplying with the variable and
taking nontangential limit at infinity that, first,
k>0
, second,
c=0
, and, from these two,

k=\frac{1}{\tau(\mathbb{A}^{2})}
. These three items of information tell us that
\mathsf{f}(iv)
tends
to infinity nontangentially as
v\to+\infty
, so that

\lim_{v\to+\infty}f(iv)=\lim_{v\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\tau\left((\mathsf{f}(iv)-\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)}-\mathsf{f}(iv)=\sphericalangle\lim_{w\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\tau\left((w-\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)}-w.

A direct computation shows that if
\tau(|\mathbb{X}|)<+\infty
, then the right hand side equals

-\tau(\mathbb{X})
. If, say, the distribution of
\mathbb{X}
with respect to
\tau
is standard Cauchy,
then the right hand side equals
i\in\mathbb{C}
, so
f(iv)\to i
as
v\to+\infty
,
and
\tau(\mathbb{X})
is not well-defined. This concludes our proof.
∎

We should mention that the pair
(\mathsf{f},f)
is uniquely detemined via Theorem
5.7

by the distributions of
\mathbb{X}
and of
(\mathbb{A,B})
with respect to the tracial state
\tau
.
Corollary
5.8
tells us then that the distribution of
\mathbb{AXA}+\mathbb{B}
with respect
to
\tau
is uniquely determined by
(\mathsf{f},f)
, so that the distribution of
\mathbb{AXA}+\mathbb{B}
with
\mathbb{X}
being *-free from
(\mathbb{A,B})
is determined via a (reasonably
simple) fixed-point functional equation involving the Cauchy transform of the distribution of

\mathbb{X}
and a variation of the Cauchy transform of the affine expression
\mathbb{B}+w\mathbb{A}^{2}
.

Remark 5.9
.

If all of
\mathbb{A,B,X}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
are positive, then of course
\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{AXA}\geq 0
as well. Then all functions in Corollary
5.8
are well-defined, analytic, and real-valued on

(-\infty,0)
. Indeed, this is immediately visible for
z\mapsto\tau\big((z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{AXA})^{-1}\big)
and for
z\mapsto\tau\big(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{AXA})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\big)
. Moreover, both these functions send
(-\infty,0)

into itself and are decreasing on
(-\infty,0)
. From (
5.54
), it follows that
\mathsf{f}

extends analytically to
(-\infty,0)
and takes values in
\mathbb{R}\setminus\sigma(\mathbb{X})
.
Combining (
5.54
) and (
5.52
), it follows that
z\mapsto f(z)
extends analytically to

(-\infty,0)
, with real values as well. Since
f\colon\mathbb{C}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}^{+}
,
it follows (for instance thanks to the Julia-Carathéodory Theorem) that
f
is strictly increasing
on
(-\infty,0)
. Corollary
5.8
together with Lindelöf’s Theorem tells us that
\lim_{v\to-\infty}f(v)=\lim_{w\to-\infty}\frac{1}{\tau((w-\mathbb{X})^{-1})}-w\in[-\infty,0)
, meaning that
if
\tau(|\mathbb{X}|)<+\infty
, then
\lim_{v\to-\infty}f(v)=-\tau(\mathbb{X})
and if
\tau(|\mathbb{X}|)=+\infty
, then
\lim_{v\to-\infty}f(v)=-\infty
. Similarly as before, the corresponding statement of
Corollary
5.8
together with Lindelöf’s Theorem imply that
\lim_{v\to-\infty}\frac{f(v)}{v}=0
.

For the purposes of studying the moments of
\mathbb{AXA}+\mathbb{B}
(those that
exist), it is convenient to use the moment generating function introduced in Section
2.2
,
and the function
\delta\colon\mathbb{C}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}^{-}
,
\delta(z)=-f(z),
and

h\colon\mathbb{C}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}^{+},h(z)=\mathsf{f}(z)+f(z)
. In the next Lemma and Example we go back to the notation form the previous Sections and consider the element
{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{B}}
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Fix a non-commutative probability space
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}},\tau)
. Let
{\mathbb{X}}
,
{\mathbb{B}}
,
{\mathbb{A}}
be self-adjoint, where
{\mathbb{A}}

is positive. Assume that
{\mathbb{X}}
and
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})
are free. Define a function
\delta\colon\mathbb{C}^{+}\cup{\mathbb{R}}^{-}\to\mathbb{C}^{-}\cup{\mathbb{R}}
as a unique analytic function satisfying a conditions

\sphericalangle\lim_{|z|\to+\infty}\frac{\delta(z)}{z}=0.

and

\psi_{{\mathbb{X}}}\left(\frac{1}{h(z)+\delta(z)}\right)=\frac{\delta(z)}{h(z)},

where

\frac{1}{h(z)}=\tau\left({\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}(z-{\mathbb{B}}-\delta(z){\mathbb{A}})^{-1}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}\right).

Then

(5.57)

\displaystyle\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{B}}}(z^{-1})=\tau\left(\left(1-\frac{\delta(z)}{z}{\mathbb{A}}-\frac{1}{z}{\mathbb{B}}\right)^{-1}\right)-1.

Proof of Lemma
5.10
.

The lemma is just a reformulation of the above remark and of Corollary
5.8
in terms of
h,\delta

and
\psi_{\mathbb{X}}
, by simply making use of the relation
\psi_{\mathbb{X}}(z)=\tau\left(z\mathbb{X}(1-z\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)=\tau\left((1-z\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)-1=\frac{1}{z}\tau\left(\left(\frac{1}{z}-\mathbb{X}\right)^{-1}\right)-1
. Indeed, the first equation is a
reformulation of (
5.52
), the second is precisely the second defining equality in Theorem

5.7
, and the third (namely (
5.57
)) is (
5.56
).
∎

Let us illustrate the behavior of function
\delta
in the example of free Beta prime distribution.

Example 5.11
.

We can calculate function
\delta
explicitly in our running example.
Recall that we have
{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{A}}
, where
{\mathbb{A}}
and
{\mathbb{X}}
are freely independent.
By (
5.57
) we have

\psi_{\mathbb{X}}(z^{-1})=\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{1+\delta(z)}{z}\right).

Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1+\delta(z)}{z}=\chi_{{\mathbb{A}}}(\psi_{{\mathbb{X}}}(z^{-1})),

with
\chi_{\mathbb{A}}=\chi_{f\beta^{\prime}_{a,a+b}}
and
\psi_{\mathbb{X}}=\psi_{f\beta^{\prime}_{a,b}}
, where by
[
37
, Proof of Proposition 3.2]
, we have

\displaystyle\psi_{f\beta^{\prime}_{a,b}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{(b-1)-(1+a)z-\sqrt{((b-1)-(1+a)z)^{2}-4az(z+1)}}{2(1+z)},

\displaystyle\chi_{f\beta^{\prime}_{a,b}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{z(b-1-z)}{(1+z)(z+a)}.

We obtain for
z<0
,
a>0
,
b\geq 1
,

\delta(z)=\frac{a\left(a-1+2b+(b-1)z+\sqrt{(z(b-1)-(1-a))^{2}-4abz}\right)}{2b(a+b-1)}.

If
b>1
, then
\lim_{z\to-\infty}\delta(z)=a/(b-1)
.
If
b=1
, then

\delta(z)=\frac{a+1+\sqrt{(1-a)^{2}-4az}}{2}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lim_{z\to-\infty}\frac{\delta(z)}{\sqrt{-z}}=\sqrt{a}.

Lemma 5.12
.

(i)

If
{\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}},{\mathbb{X}}\geq 0
, then
\delta\colon(-\infty,0)\to(0,+\infty)
is monotonic.

(ii)

If
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}({\mathbb{A}},-{\mathbb{B}})
and
{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-{\mathbb{X}}
, then
\delta\colon i(-\infty,0)\to i(0,+\infty)
is monotonic.

Proof.

Part (i) has been shown as part of Remark
5.9
. Part (ii) follows from Theorem
5.7

by observing that
\Re\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(iy-\mathbb{B}+iv\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)=\Re\tau\left((iy-\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)=0
for all
y,v>0
.
∎

To conclude this section, observe that
\mathbb{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{AXA}

means that

\displaystyle\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{AXA})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\left((\mathsf{f}(z)-\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\left((\mathsf{f}(z)-\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{AXA})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau\left((\mathsf{f}(z)-\mathbb{B}+f(\mathsf{f}(z))\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\right),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}.

At the same time, Theorem
5.7
tells us also that
\tau\left((\mathsf{f}(z)-\mathbb{X})^{-1}\right)=\frac{1}{\mathsf{f}(z)+f(z)}
. Thus, the functions
(\mathsf{f},f)
corresponding to a free perpetuity
must satisfy

\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)=\frac{1}{\mathsf{f}(z)+f(z)}=\tau\left((\mathsf{f}(z)-\mathbb{B}+f(\mathsf{f}(z))\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\right).

Unfortunately, there seems to be no direct way to determine the existence of such a pair

(\mathsf{f},f)
from these two equalities. They are equivalent to the pair

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+f(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-f(z),

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau\left(({\sf f}(z)-\mathbb{B}+f(\mathsf{f}(z))\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\right)}-{\sf f}(z).

Given a function
f_{0}\colon\mathbb{C}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}^{+}
such that

\lim_{y\to+\infty}\frac{f_{0}(iy)}{iy}=0
, one defines

\mathsf{f}_{1}(z)=\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+f_{0}(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-f_{0}(z),

and

f_{1}(z)=\frac{1}{\tau\left(({\sf f}_{1}(z)-\mathbb{B}+f_{0}(\mathsf{f}_{1}(z))\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\right)}-{\sf f}_{1}(z).

It is clear that
\lim_{y\to+\infty}\frac{\mathsf{f}_{1}(iy)}{iy}=\frac{1}{\tau(\mathbb{A}^{2})}
and

\lim_{y\to+\infty}\frac{f_{1}(iy)}{iy}=0
. Inductively, given
(\mathsf{f}_{j},f_{j}),1\leq j\leq n,
satisfying
these same conditions, we let

\mathsf{f}_{n+1}(z)=\frac{1}{\tau\left(\mathbb{A}(z-\mathbb{B}+f_{n}(z)\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{A}\right)}-f_{n}(z),

and

f_{n+1}(z)=\frac{1}{\tau\left(({\sf f}_{n}(z)-\mathbb{B}+f_{n-1}(\mathsf{f}_{n}(z))\mathbb{A}^{2})^{-1}\right)}-{\sf f}_{n}(z).

We conjecture that
(\mathsf{f}_{n},f_{n})\to(\mathsf{f},f)
as
n\to+\infty
in the topology of uniform
convergence on compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}^{+}
, where
(\mathsf{f},f)
are the functions
provided by Corollary
5.8
with
\mathbb{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{AXA}

if and only if
\tau(\mathbb{A}^{2})\leq 1
and
\tau(\log^{+}{\mathbb{B}})<+\infty
.

6.
Tails of the solution to the affine fixed-point equation

In this section using the analytic tools developed in the previous sections we are able to determine the behavior of the tails of free perpetuities.

Theorem 6.1
.

Assume
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
, where
{\mathbb{A}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
is non-Dirac and
{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
, with
{\mathbb{B}}\geq 0
and

{\mathbb{B}}\neq 0
. If the model (
4.7
) is irreducible, then the unique solution
{\mathbb{X}}
to
(
4.7
) exists, and moreover:

(i)

if
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})<+\infty
, then

(6.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}t^{1/2}\mu_{{\mathbb{X}}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}}.

(ii)

if
\mu_{\mathbb{A}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
c>0
, then

(6.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}t^{1/\alpha}\mu_{{\mathbb{X}}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\frac{\sin(\pi/\alpha)}{\pi/\alpha}\left(\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{\pi\,c}\tau({\mathbb{B}})\right)^{1/\alpha}.

Theorem 6.2
.

Assume
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
, where
{\mathbb{A}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
is non-Dirac, and
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}({\mathbb{A}},-{\mathbb{B}})
with

{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
. If the model (
4.7
) is irreducible, then the unique solution
{\mathbb{X}}
to
(
4.7
) exists, and moreover:

(i)

if
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})<+\infty
, then

\lim_{t\to+\infty}t\,\mu_{|{\mathbb{X}}|}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}}.

(ii)

if
\mu_{\mathbb{A}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
c>0
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}t^{2/\alpha}\,\mu_{|{\mathbb{X}}|}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\frac{\sin(\pi/\alpha)}{\pi/\alpha}\left(\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{2\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})\right)^{1/\alpha}.

6.1.
Examples

We continue with both examples from Section
4.1
to illustrate our results.

6.1.1.
Free Beta prime distribution

Let
a>0
,
b\geq 1
and assume that
{\mathbb{A}}\sim f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,a+b}
. By Corollary
4.1
,

{\mathbb{X}}\sim f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,b}
is a solution to

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{A}},\qquad{\mathbb{A}}\mbox{ and }{\mathbb{X}}\mbox{ are freely independent}.

We have
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=a/(a+b-1)<1
if and only if
b>1
. In this case, the support of
{\mathbb{X}}
is compact.

If
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
, then
b=1
, and thus
{\mathbb{X}}\sim f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,1}
.
Since
S_{\mathbb{X}}(-1/x)=1/(ax-1)\sim a^{-1}x^{-1}
as
x\to+\infty
, we can apply

[
26
, Theorem 4.5]
for
\alpha=1/2
to obtain

(6.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}t^{1/2}\mu_{{\mathbb{X}}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\frac{2\sqrt{a}}{\pi}.

Notice that for
a>0
and
b>1
, we have
m_{1}(f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,b})=\lim_{z\to 0-}S_{a,b}(z)^{-1}=\frac{a}{b-1}
and (recalling (
2.1
))

\mathrm{Var}(f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,b})=-m_{1}(f\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{a,b})^{3}\lim_{z\to 0-}S_{a,b}^{\prime}(z)=\frac{a(a+b-1)}{(b-1)^{3}}.

Thus, for
b=1
, we find that
\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})=2/a
, which allows us to express the right-hand
side of (
6.3
) as
\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{\tau({\mathbb{A}})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}}
.

6.1.2.
Inverse Marchenko-Pastur distribution

In Section
4.1.2
, we established that if
\lambda\geq 1
and
{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}\sim fGIG_{-\lambda}
,
then
{\mathbb{X}}\sim\mu_{\lambda}^{-1}
is a solution to

{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2},\qquad{\mathbb{A}}\mbox{ and }{\mathbb{X}}\mbox{ are free}.

When
\lambda>1
, then
\tau({\mathbb{A}})<1
, resulting in a compact support of
{\mathbb{X}}
.

Conversely, if
\lambda=1
, then
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
. By direct calculation using (
4.6
), we
may prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}t^{1/2}\mu_{{\mathbb{X}}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)=\frac{2}{\pi}.

Furthermore, by performing a series expansion of the Cauchy transform of
{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}
(see, e.g.,

[
32
, page 380]
), we can derive formulas for
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})
and
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{1/2})
in terms of
the endpoints
(a,b)
of the support of
{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}
. Using the system of equations that define these
endpoints, we find that
\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})=2\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{1/2})
. This leads to the following equality:

\frac{2}{\pi}=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{1/2})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}}.

6.2.
Proofs

Let

\displaystyle\beta(z)

\displaystyle=\tau\left(\left(1-\frac{\delta(z)}{z}{\mathbb{A}}-\frac{1}{z}{\mathbb{B}}\right)^{-1}\right)-1

for
z\in(-\infty,0)
in the case of positive
{\mathbb{B}}
(Subsection
6.2.1
) and for
z\in i(-\infty,0)
in
the case of symmetric
{\mathbb{B}}
(Subsection
6.2.2
). By Lemma
5.10
, we have

\displaystyle\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{B}}}(z^{-1})=\beta(z)\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\psi_{{\mathbb{X}}}\left(\frac{1}{h(z)+\delta(z)}\right)=\frac{\delta(z)}{h(z)},

where

\frac{1}{h(z)}=\tau\left({\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}(z-{\mathbb{B}}-\delta(z){\mathbb{A}})^{-1}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}\right).

If
{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}+{\mathbb{B}}
, then we additionally have
\beta(z)=\psi_{\mathbb{X}}(z^{-1})
.
Thus, we obtain

(6.4)

\displaystyle\beta\left(h(z)+\delta(z)\right)=\frac{\delta(z)}{h(z)}.

We begin with a series of technical lemmas that will be utilized in the proofs of Theorem

6.1
and Theorem
6.2
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Assume that
{\mathbb{A}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
,
{\mathbb{A}}\geq 0
and
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
.

(i)

If
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})<+\infty
, Then, as
z\to 0-
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\psi_{\mathbb{A}}(z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{z}-\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})(1+o(1)).

(ii)

If
\mu_{\mathbb{A}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
c>0
, then
as
z\to 0-
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(z\right)}=\frac{1}{z}+c\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}(-z)^{\alpha-2}(1+o(1)).

(iii)

If
a_{n}\to 0-
and
b_{n}\to 0-
, then

\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}(a_{n})-\psi_{\mathbb{A}}(b_{n})=(a_{n}-b_{n})(1+o(1)).

Proof.

(i)

We have
\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}(z)=z+\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})z^{2}(1+o(1))
. Therefore,

\frac{1}{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}(z)}=\frac{1}{z}\frac{1}{1+\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})z(1+o(1)}=\frac{1}{z}(1-\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})z(1+o(1))).

(ii)

By Theorem
2.2
we obtain

\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(z\right)=z-c\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}(-z)^{\alpha}(1+o(1)).

Similarly to (i), we arrive at

\frac{1}{\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(z\right)}=\frac{1}{z}\left(1-c\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}(-z)^{\alpha-1}(1+o(1))\right)

(iii)

We have

\displaystyle\frac{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}(a_{n})-\psi_{\mathbb{A}}(b_{n})}{a_{n}-b_{n}}=\int_{[0,+\infty)}\frac{x}{(1-a_{n}x)(1-b_{n}x)}\mu_{{\mathbb{A}}}(\mathrm{d}x).

Since both
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
are negative, we have
\frac{x}{(1-a_{n}x)(1-b_{n}x)}\leq x\in L^{1}(\mu_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathrm{d}x))
. By the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, we obtain the assertion.

∎

6.2.1.
Positive
{\mathbb{B}}

Now, we formulate and prove lemmas which will be applicable in the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Assume
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
,
{\mathbb{A}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
is non-Dirac and
{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
is such that
{\mathbb{B}}\geq 0
and
{\mathbb{B}}\neq 0
.
Additionally, suppose that the model (
4.7
) is irreducible. As
z\to-\infty
, we have

(i)

\delta(z)\to+\infty
,

(ii)

\beta(z)=\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)+\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{z}(1+o(1))
,

(iii)

\frac{1}{h(z)}=\frac{1}{\delta(z)}\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)
.

Proof.

(i)

Since for
t\geq 0
, we have
(1+t)^{-1}-1\geq-t
, we get for
z<0
,

\left(1-\frac{\delta(z)}{z}{\mathbb{A}}-\frac{1}{z}{\mathbb{B}}\right)^{-1}-1\geq\frac{\delta(z)}{z}{\mathbb{A}}+\frac{1}{z}{\mathbb{B}}.

Applying
\tau
to both sides and multiplying by
z<0
, we obtain
z\,\beta(z)\leq\delta(z)+\tau({\mathbb{B}})
.

Since
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
and
\tau({\mathbb{B}})>0
, (
4.7
) leads to
\tau({\mathbb{X}})=\tau({\mathbb{X}})+\tau({\mathbb{B}})
, which is only possible if
\tau({\mathbb{X}})=+\infty
. Therefore,

\lim_{z\to-\infty}z\,\psi_{\mathbb{X}}(z^{-1})=\tau({\mathbb{X}})=+\infty.

Using the fact that
\psi_{\mathbb{X}}(z^{-1})=\beta(z)
(recall Lemma
5.10
), we deduce that
\delta(z)\to+\infty
as
z\to-\infty
.

(ii)

Let
a=1-z^{-1}\delta(z){\mathbb{A}}
and
b=-z^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}
. Note that we have
a\geq 1
and
b\geq 0
. Let
f(x)=x/(1+x)
.
Then,

\displaystyle z\left(\left(1-\frac{\delta(z)}{z}{\mathbb{A}}-\frac{1}{z}{\mathbb{B}}\right)^{-1}-\left(1-\frac{\delta(z)}{z}{\mathbb{A}}\right)^{-1}\right)=z\left((a+b)^{-1}-a^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=-za^{-1/2}f(a^{-1/2}ba^{-1/2})a^{-1/2}.

For
x\geq 0
, we have
x-x^{2}\leq f(x)\leq x
. Therefore, for
z<0
,

\displaystyle-za^{-1}ba^{-1}+za^{-1}ba^{-1}ba^{-1}\leq-za^{-1/2}f(a^{-1/2}ba^{-1/2})a^{-1/2}\leq-za^{-1}ba^{-1}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle-za^{-1}ba^{-1}=a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}a^{-1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad za^{-1}ba^{-1}ba^{-1}=\frac{1}{z}a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}a^{-1}.

Since
{\mathbb{B}}
is bounded and
a\geq 1
,
a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}a^{-1}
is also bounded. Therefore,

\lim_{z\to-\infty}z\,\tau(a^{-1}ba^{-1}ba^{-1})=0.

Furthermore,

\lim_{z\to-\infty}(-z)\tau(a^{-1}ba^{-1})=\lim_{z\to-\infty}\tau(a^{-2}{\mathbb{B}})=\tau({\mathbb{B}}),

because both
a^{-1}
and
{\mathbb{B}}
are bounded and
a\to 1
, which follows from
\delta(z)/z\to 0
as
z\to-\infty
. Thus, (ii) follows.

(iii)

With the notation from the previous part, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h(z)}-\frac{1}{\delta(z)}\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)

\displaystyle=\tau\left({\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}\left[(z-{\mathbb{B}}-\delta(z){\mathbb{A}})^{-1}-(z-\delta(z){\mathbb{A}})^{-1}\right]{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{-z}\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}a^{-1/2}f(a^{-1/2}ba^{-1/2})a^{-1/2}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{-z}\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}a^{-1}ba^{-1}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2})=\frac{1}{z^{2}}\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{1/2}a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}a^{-1}{\mathbb{A}}^{1/2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{z^{2}}\tau({\mathbb{A}}a^{-2})\|{\mathbb{B}}\|\leq\frac{1}{z^{2}}\tau({\mathbb{A}})\|{\mathbb{B}}\|.

The left-hand side above is nonnegative, completing the proof.

∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Assume
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
,
{\mathbb{A}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
is non-Dirac and
{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
is such that
{\mathbb{B}}\geq 0
and
{\mathbb{B}}\neq 0
. Additionally, assume that the model (
4.7
) is irreducible.

(i)

If
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})<+\infty
, then as
z\to-\infty
,

h(z)=z-\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})\delta(z)(1+o(1)).

(ii)

If
\mu_{\mathbb{A}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
c>0
, then as
z\to-\infty
,

h(z)=z-c\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}z\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{-z}\right)^{\alpha-1}(1+o(1)).

Proof.

We write

h(z)-z=\left(h(z)-\frac{\delta(z)}{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)}\right)+\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)}-z\right).

By Lemma
6.4
, we have
\delta(z)h(z)\sim\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)
, and thus,

h(z)-\frac{\delta(z)}{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)}=\frac{h(z)\delta(z)}{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)}\left(\frac{1}{\delta(z)}\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)-\frac{1}{h(z)}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right).

(i)

Since
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})<+\infty
, by Lemma
6.3
(i), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\delta(z)}{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)}-z=-\delta(z)\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})(1+o(1)).

(ii)

By Lemma
6.3
(ii), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\delta(z)}{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)}-z

\displaystyle=c\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}\delta(z)\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{-z}\right)^{\alpha-2}(1+o(1)).

Since
\delta(z)=o(z)
, we conclude (ii).

∎

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

By Theorem
2.2
(i), the expressions (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) are equivalent to the following, respectively, as
t\to+\infty
,

\displaystyle-\psi_{\mathbb{X}}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)

\displaystyle\sim\sqrt{\frac{2\,\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}}\frac{1}{t^{1/2}},

\displaystyle-\psi_{\mathbb{X}}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}})\right)^{1/\alpha}\frac{1}{t^{1/\alpha}}.

By Lemma
6.4
(iii), we have
\beta(z)\sim\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{z}\right)\sim\frac{\delta(z)}{z}
. Since
\psi_{\mathbb{X}}(z^{-1})=\beta(z)
, to complete the proof, it is sufficient to show that, as
t\to+\infty
,

(6.5)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta(-t)}{t}

\displaystyle\sim\sqrt{\frac{2\,\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}}\frac{1}{t^{1/2}},

(6.6)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta(-t)}{t}

\displaystyle\sim\left(\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}})\right)^{1/\alpha}\frac{1}{t^{1/\alpha}},

respectively. By Lemma
6.4
, we already know that
\delta(z)\to+\infty
as
z\to-\infty
.

For a fixed
z_{0}<0
, we define a sequence
(z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
by

z_{n}=h(z_{n-1})+\delta(z_{n-1}),

and denote
h_{n}=h(z_{n})
and
\delta_{n}=\delta(z_{n})
for
n=1,2,\ldots
. We will first show (
6.5
) and (
6.6
) hold for this sequence.

First, we prove (i). By Lemma
6.5
(i), we have

h(z)-z\sim-\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})\delta(z),

which implies that there exists
z_{0}<0
such that for any
z<z_{0}
,

(6.7)

\displaystyle z-h(z)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})\delta(z)>0.

This clearly implies that

z_{n-1}-z_{n}=z_{n-1}-h_{n-1}-\delta_{n-1}\geq((1-\varepsilon)\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})-1)\delta_{n-1}=(\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})-\varepsilon\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2}))\delta_{n-1}>0

for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
. Thus, the sequence
(z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is decreasing and has a limit, say
g\leq z_{0}
. We conclude that
g=-\infty
, because if
g\in(-\infty,0)
, this would imply that
\delta_{n}\to 0
, which is impossible. Hence,
z_{n}\to-\infty
as
n\to+\infty
.

The sequences
(z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(\delta_{n})_{n\geq 1}
satisfy the following properties:

(a)

z_{n-1}-z_{n}\sim\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})\delta_{n-1}
; thus,
z_{n-1}\sim z_{n}
,

(b)

\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}-\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\sim-\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{z_{n}}
; thus,
\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\sim\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}
,

(c)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\delta_{n}^{2}}{z_{n}}=-\frac{2\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}
.

Property (a) follows from Lemma
6.5
(i).
By (
6.4
), we have
\beta(z_{n})=\delta_{n-1}/h_{n-1}
. By Lemma
6.4
and the fact that
z_{n-1}\sim z_{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{h_{n-1}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\right)+O\left(\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}^{2}}\right)=\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\right)+o\left(\frac{1}{z_{n}}\right),

\displaystyle\beta(z_{n})

\displaystyle=\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\right)+\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{z_{n}}(1+o(1)).

Thus, by Lemma
6.3
(iii), we get

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}-\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\sim\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\right)-\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\right)\sim-\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{z_{n}},

which establishes property (b).

By properties (a) and (b), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\right)^{2}}{\frac{1}{z_{n}}-\frac{1}{z_{n-1}}}\sim\frac{\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}-\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\right)2\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}}{(z_{n-1}-z_{n})\frac{1}{z_{n}^{2}}}\sim\frac{\left(-\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{z_{n}}\right)2\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})\delta_{n-1}\frac{1}{z_{n}^{2}}}\sim-\frac{2\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}.

The Stolz–Cesàro theorem implies (c). Indeed, since
(z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is monotonic and diverging, we obtain

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\right)^{2}}{\frac{1}{z_{n}}}=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\right)^{2}}{\frac{1}{z_{n}}-\frac{1}{z_{n-1}}}.

Next, we show that
\lim_{z\to-\infty}\frac{\delta(z)^{2}}{z}=-\frac{2\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}
.
Since the function
(-\infty,z_{0})\ni z\mapsto\delta(z)
is monotonic and diverges to
+\infty
as
z\to-\infty
, for
z\in[z_{n},z_{n-1})
, we have

\delta_{n-1}\leq\delta(z)\leq\delta_{n}

which leads to

\frac{z_{n}}{z_{n-1}}\frac{\delta_{n}^{2}}{z_{n}}\leq\frac{\delta(z)^{2}}{z}\leq\frac{z_{n-1}}{z_{n}}\frac{\delta_{n-1}^{2}}{z_{n-1}}.

Since
z_{n}\sim z_{n-1}
, we get

\limsup_{z\to-\infty}\frac{\delta(z)^{2}}{z}\leq\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\frac{z_{n-1}}{z_{n}}\frac{\delta_{n-1}^{2}}{z_{n-1}}=-\frac{2\,\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}.

A similar argument can be applied to the
\liminf
.
Therefore, we conclude that as
z\to-\infty
,

\delta(z)\sim\sqrt{-\frac{2\,\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}z},

which is equivalent to (
6.5
). This completes the proof of (i).

We now turn to point (ii). Similarly as before, Lemma
6.5
(ii) implies that there exists
z_{0}<0
such that the sequence
(z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is decreasing and
z_{n}\to-\infty
as
n\to+\infty
.

We will show that the sequences
(z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(\delta_{n})_{n\geq 1}
satisfy the following properties:

(a’)

z_{n-1}-z_{n}\sim c\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}\left(\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{-z_{n-1}}\right)^{\alpha-1}z_{n-1}
; thus,
z_{n}\sim z_{n-1}
,

(b’)

\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}-\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\sim-\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{z_{n}}
; thus
\frac{\delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\sim\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}
,

(c’)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}z_{n}\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{-z_{n}}\right)^{\alpha}=\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha)}{\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}})
.

Property (a’) follows from Lemma
6.5
(ii), while property (b’) mirrors our earlier findings.

Now we calculate

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{-z_{n}}\right)^{\alpha}-\left(\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{-z_{n-1}}\right)^{\alpha}}{\frac{1}{z_{n}}-\frac{1}{z_{n-1}}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\alpha\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{-z_{n}}\right)^{\alpha-1}\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{-z_{n}}-\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{-z_{n-1}}\right)}{(z_{n-1}-z_{n})\frac{1}{z_{n}^{2}}}\sim\frac{\alpha\left(\frac{\delta_{n}}{-z_{n}}\right)^{\alpha-1}\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}})}{z_{n}}}{c\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}\left(\frac{\delta_{n-1}}{-z_{n-1}}\right)^{\alpha-1}z_{n-1}\frac{1}{z_{n}^{2}}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha)}{\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}}).

Thus, by the Stolz–Cesàro theorem, we obtain (c’).
Arguing similarly as before, we find that

\lim_{z\to-\infty}z\left(\frac{\delta(z)}{-z}\right)^{\alpha}=\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha)}{\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}}),

which is (
6.6
). This concludes the proof.
∎

6.2.2.
Symmetric
{\mathbb{B}}

Denote for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\Delta(t)=\delta(it)/i.

Then, for
z<0
,

\beta(iz)=\tau\left(\left(1-\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}{\mathbb{A}}+\frac{i}{z}{\mathbb{B}}\right)^{-1}\right)-1.

Under the assumption that
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}({\mathbb{A}},-{\mathbb{B}})
and
{\mathbb{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-{\mathbb{X}}
, the function
(-\infty,0)\ni z\mapsto\beta(iz)
is real. In this case, we have

\beta(iz)=\tau\left(\Re\left(\left(1-\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}{\mathbb{A}}+\frac{i}{z}{\mathbb{B}}\right)^{-1}\right)\right)-1.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Re(t)\geq 0
. Then,

\Re\left(\frac{1}{1+t}\right)\geq 1-\Re(t)-\Im(t)^{2}.

Proof.

Let
t=x+iy
with
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then,

\Re\left(\frac{1}{1+t}\right)=\frac{1+x}{(1+x)^{2}+y^{2}}.

The inequality

1+x\geq(1-x-y^{2})\left((1+x)^{2}+y^{2}\right)

is equivalent to

x^{2}(1+x)+x(3+x)y^{2}+y^{4}\geq 0,

which is clearly satisfied for all
x\geq 0
.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Assume
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}({\mathbb{A}},-{\mathbb{B}})
,
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
,
{\mathbb{A}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
is non-Dirac and
{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
with
{\mathbb{B}}\neq 0
. Additionally, suppose that the model (
4.7
) is irreducible. As
z\to-\infty
, we have

(i)

z\Delta(z)\to-\infty
,

(ii)

\beta(iz)=\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}\right)-\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{z^{2}}(1+o(1))
,

(iii)

\frac{i}{h(iz)}=\frac{1}{\Delta(z)}\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{z^{3}}\right)
.

Proof.

(i)

Since
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
and
{\mathbb{B}}\neq 0
, (
4.7
) implies that
\tau({\mathbb{X}}^{2})=+\infty
. Therefore,

\lim_{z\to-\infty}z^{2}\psi_{\mathbb{X}}\left(\frac{1}{iz}\right)=\lim_{z\to-\infty}z^{2}\psi_{{\mathbb{X}}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{-z^{2}}\right)=-\tau({\mathbb{X}}^{2})=-\infty.

Applying Lemma
6.6
to
t=-\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}{\mathbb{A}}+\frac{i}{z}{\mathbb{B}}
for
z<0
and taking
\tau
of both sides, we arrive at

\beta(iz)\geq\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2}).

Since
\beta(iz)=\psi_{\mathbb{X}}((iz)^{-1})
, we obtain

-\infty=\lim_{z\to-\infty}z^{2}\beta(iz)\geq\limsup_{z\to+\infty}\left(z\Delta(z)+\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})\right),

which establishes (i).

(ii)

Let
a=1-z^{-1}\Delta(z){\mathbb{A}}
and
b=z^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}
. Note that
a\geq 1
for
z<0
. Let
f(x)=x/(1+x)
.
Then,

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}{\mathbb{A}}+\frac{i}{z}{\mathbb{B}}\right)^{-1}-\left(1-\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}{\mathbb{A}}\right)^{-1}=(a+ib)^{-1}-a^{-1}

\displaystyle=-a^{-1/2}f(ia^{-1/2}ba^{-1/2})a^{-1/2}

For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
x^{2}-x^{4}\leq\Re(f(ix))=f(x^{2})\leq x^{2}
. Therefore, for
z<0
,

\displaystyle a^{-1}ba^{-1}ba^{-1}-(a^{-1}b)^{4}a^{-1}\leq a^{-1/2}\Re\left(f(ia^{-1/2}ba^{-1/2})a^{-1/2}\right)\leq a^{-1}ba^{-1}ba^{-1}.

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle a^{-1}ba^{-1}ba^{-1}=\frac{1}{z^{2}}a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}a^{-1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad(a^{-1}b)^{4}a^{-1}=\frac{1}{z^{4}}(a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}})^{4}a^{-1}.

Clearly,
(a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}})^{4}a^{-1}
is bounded because
{\mathbb{B}}
is bounded and
a\geq 1
.
Therefore

\lim_{z\to-\infty}z^{2}\,\tau\left((a^{-1}b)^{4}a^{-1}\right)=0.

Moreover, since
\Delta(z)/z\to 0
, we have
a\to 1
. Therefore,

\lim_{z\to-\infty}z^{2}\tau\left(a^{-1}ba^{-1}ba^{-1}\right)=\lim_{z\to-\infty}\tau(a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}a^{-1}{\mathbb{B}}a^{-1})=\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})<+\infty.

This gives (ii).

(iii)

We leave the proof of (iii) to the reader, as it is similar to the proof of the Lemma
6.4
(iii).

∎

Lemma 6.8
.

Assume
({\mathbb{A}},{\mathbb{B}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}({\mathbb{A}},-{\mathbb{B}})
,
\tau({\mathbb{A}})=1
,
{\mathbb{A}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
is non-Dirac and
{\mathbb{B}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
with
{\mathbb{B}}\neq 0
. Additionally, assume that the model (
4.7
) is irreducible.

(i)

If
\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})<+\infty
, then as
z\to-\infty
,

\frac{1}{i}h(iz)=z-\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})\Delta(z)(1+o(1)).

(ii)

If
\mu_{\mathbb{A}}\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
c>0
, then as
z\to-\infty
,

\frac{1}{i}h(iz)=z-c\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}z\left(\frac{\Delta(z)}{-z}\right)^{\alpha-1}(1+o(1)).

Proof.

We write

\frac{1}{i}h(iz)-z=\left(\frac{1}{i}h(iz)-\frac{\Delta(z)}{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}\right)}\right)+\left(\frac{\Delta(z)}{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}\right)}-z\right).

By Lemma
6.7
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{i}h(iz)-\frac{\Delta(z)}{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}h(iz)\frac{\Delta(z)}{\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}\right)}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta(z)}\psi_{{\mathbb{A}}}\left(\frac{\Delta(z)}{z}\right)-\frac{i}{h(iz)}\right)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{z^{3}}\tau({\mathbb{A}}{\mathbb{B}}^{2}).

Similarly as in the proof of Lemma
6.5
, by applying (i) and (ii) from Lemma
6.3
, we obtain the assertion.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.2
.

Define a sequence
(z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
by

z_{n}=\frac{h(iz_{n-1})}{i}+\Delta(z_{n-1}).

Note that all
z_{n}
are real. Following the arguments in the proof of Theorem
6.1
, we can show that
(z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is monotonic and diverges to
-\infty
.
Denote
\Delta_{n}=\Delta(z_{n})
for
n\geq 1
.

First, consider (i).
We will prove that the sequences
(z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(\Delta_{n})_{n\geq 1}
satisfy the following properties:

(a)

z_{n-1}-z_{n}\sim\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})\Delta_{n-1}
; thus,
z_{n-1}\sim z_{n}
,

(b)

\frac{\Delta_{n}}{z_{n}}-\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\sim\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{z_{n}^{2}}
; thus,
\frac{\Delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\sim\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}
,

(c)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\Delta_{n}^{2}=\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}
.

By Lemma
6.8
(i), we have

z_{n}-z_{n-1}=\frac{h(iz_{n-1})}{i}-z_{n-1}+\Delta_{n-1}\sim-\tau({\mathbb{A}}^{2})\Delta_{n-1}(1+o(1))+\tau({\mathbb{A}})^{2}\Delta_{n-1},

which establishes (a). Using (
6.4
), we get

\beta(iz_{n})=\frac{i\Delta(z_{n-1})}{h(iz_{n-1})}.

From Lemma
6.7
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{i\,\Delta(z_{n-1})}{h(iz_{n-1})}

\displaystyle=\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{n-1}z_{n-1}^{3}}\right)=\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\right)+o\left(\frac{1}{z_{n}^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\beta(iz_{n})

\displaystyle=\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\right)-\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{z_{n}^{2}}(1+o(1)).

Thus, by Lemma
6.3
(iii), we get

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta_{n}}{z_{n}}-\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\sim\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\right)-\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\left(\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\right)=\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{z_{n}^{2}}(1+o(1)),

which establishes (b).

Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\right)^{2}}{\frac{1}{z_{n}^{2}}-\frac{1}{z_{n-1}^{2}}}\sim\frac{\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{z_{n}}-\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\right)2\frac{\Delta_{n}}{z_{n}}}{(z_{n-1}-z_{n})\frac{2}{z_{n}^{3}}}\sim\frac{\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{z_{n}^{2}}2\frac{\Delta_{n}}{z_{n}}}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})\Delta_{n-1}\frac{2}{z_{n}^{3}}}\sim\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}.

The Stolz–Cesàro theorem implies property (c).
Since the function
(-\infty,0)\ni z\mapsto\Delta(z)
is decreasing, for
z\in[z_{n},z_{n-1})
, we have
\Delta_{n-1}\leq\Delta(z)\leq\Delta_{n}
. Consequently, we obtain

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\Delta_{n-1}\leq\liminf_{z\to-\infty}\Delta(z)\leq\limsup_{z\to+\infty}\Delta(z)\leq\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\Delta_{n}.

This implies that

\lim_{z\to-\infty}\Delta(z)=\sqrt{\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}}.

Thus, we find

-\psi_{\mathbb{X}}\left(-\frac{1}{it}\right)=-\beta(-it)\sim\frac{\Delta(-t)}{t}\sim\sqrt{\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}}\frac{1}{t}.

By Theorem
2.3
for
\alpha=1
and
L\equiv\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{A}})}}
, we conclude the assertion.

We now prove (ii). The sequences
(z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(\Delta_{n})_{n\geq 1}
satisfy the following properties

(a’)

z_{n-1}-z_{n}\sim c\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}\left(\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{-z_{n-1}}\right)^{\alpha-1}z_{n-1}
; thus,
z_{n-1}\sim z_{n}
,

(b’)

\frac{\Delta_{n}}{z_{n}}-\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}\sim\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{z_{n}^{2}}
; thus,
\frac{\Delta_{n}}{z_{n}}\sim\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{z_{n-1}}
,

(c’)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}z_{n}^{2}\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{-z_{n}}\right)^{\alpha}=\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{2\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})
.

Property (a’) follows from Lemma
6.5
(ii), while property (b’) coincides with (b) from part (i). By properties (a’) and (b’), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{-z_{n}}\right)^{\alpha}-\left(\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{-z_{n-1}}\right)^{\alpha}}{\frac{1}{z_{n}^{2}}-\frac{1}{z_{n-1}^{2}}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\alpha\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{-z_{n}}\right)^{\alpha-1}\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{-z_{n}}-\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{-z_{n-1}}\right)}{(z_{n-1}-z_{n})\frac{2}{z_{n}^{3}}}\sim\frac{-\alpha\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{-z_{n}}\right)^{\alpha-1}\frac{\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})}{z_{n}^{2}}}{c\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)}\left(\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{-z_{n-1}}\right)^{\alpha-1}z_{n-1}\frac{2}{z_{n}^{3}}},

which simplifies to

\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{2\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2}).

By the Stolz–Cesàro theorem, we obtain (c’).

For
z\in[z_{n},z_{n-1})
, we have

z_{n-1}^{2}\left(\frac{\Delta_{n-1}}{-z_{n}}\right)^{\alpha}\leq z^{2}\left(\frac{\Delta(z)}{-z}\right)^{\alpha}\leq z_{n}^{2}\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{-z_{n-1}}\right)^{\alpha}.

Since
z_{n}\sim z_{n-1}
, using (c’), we conclude as
z\to-\infty
,

\Delta(z)\sim\left(\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{2\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})\right)^{1/\alpha}\frac{1}{(-z)^{2/\alpha-1}}.

Thus, we find that

-\psi_{\mathbb{X}}\left(-\frac{1}{it}\right)\sim\frac{\Delta(-t)}{t}\sim\left(\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{2\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})\right)^{1/\alpha}\frac{1}{t^{2/\alpha}}.

Applying Theorem
2.3
with index
2/\alpha\in(1,2)
and
L\equiv\frac{\sin(\pi/\alpha)}{\pi/\alpha}\left(\frac{-\sin(\pi\alpha)}{2\pi c}\tau({\mathbb{B}}^{2})\right)^{1/\alpha}
, we conclude the result.
∎

Appendix

In the Appendix, we provide several supplementary results related to the limit theorem introduced in
[
31
]
. In particular, we prove that the convergence, originally established in distribution, can be strengthened to hold in
L^{2}
.

Theorem 6.9
.

Assume that
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
is non-Dirac, and let
(\Pi_{n,k})_{1\leq k\leq n}
be freely independent copies of
\Pi_{n}\sim\mu^{\boxtimes n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(i)

If
m_{2}(\mu)<+\infty
, define

{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}=\frac{1}{n\,m_{1}(\mu)^{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Pi_{n,k}.

Then,
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}
converges in distribution to a random variable
{\mathbb{Y}}
with
S_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(z)=\exp(-\eta z)
,
z\in(-1,0)
, where
\eta=\mathrm{Var}(\mu)/m_{1}(\mu)^{2}
.

(ii)

If
\mu\big((t,+\infty)\big)\sim c\,t^{-\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(1,2)
, define

{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}=\frac{1}{n^{\beta}m_{1}(\mu)^{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor n^{\beta}\rfloor}\Pi_{n,k},

where
\beta=1/(\alpha-1)
. Then,
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}
converges in distribution to a random variable
{\mathbb{Y}}^{(\alpha)}
with

S_{{\mathbb{Y}}^{(\alpha)}}(z)=\exp(\eta_{\alpha}(-z)^{\alpha-1})
,
z\in(-1,0)
, where

\eta_{\alpha}=c\frac{\pi\alpha}{\sin(\pi\alpha)m_{1}(\mu)^{\alpha}}.

Proof.

The first part was proved in
[
31
, Theorem 3.3]
.
The distribution
\mu_{n}^{\alpha}
of
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}^{(\alpha)}
can be expressed as

\mu_{n}^{\alpha}=D_{\tfrac{1}{n^{\beta}m_{1}(\mu)^{n}}}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})^{\boxplus\lfloor n^{\beta}\rfloor},

where
D_{a}
,
a>0
, is a dilation operator defined by
D_{a}\mu(\cdot)=\mu(a^{-1}\cdot)
.
By
[
6
, (3.7) and (3.11)]
, we have

S_{D_{a}\mu}=\frac{1}{a}S_{\mu}\quad\mbox{and}\quad S_{\mu^{\boxplus n}}(z)=\frac{1}{n}S_{\mu}\left(\frac{z}{n}\right).

Moreover, by
[
26
, Theorem 4.9 and Remark 4.10]
applied to
L\equiv c
, we obtain, as
z\to 0^{-}
,

S_{\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{m_{1}(\mu)}\left(1+\eta_{\alpha}(-z)^{\alpha-1}(1+o(1))\right).

Thus,

\displaystyle S_{\mu_{n}^{\alpha}}(z)

\displaystyle=n^{\beta}m_{1}(\mu)^{n}S_{(\mu^{\boxtimes n})^{\boxplus\lfloor n^{\beta}\rfloor}}(z)=\frac{n^{\beta}}{\lfloor n^{\beta}\rfloor}m_{1}(\mu)^{n}S_{\mu^{\boxtimes n}}\left(\frac{z}{\lfloor n^{\beta}\rfloor}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{\beta}}{\lfloor n^{\beta}\rfloor}m_{1}(\mu)^{n}S_{\mu}\left(\frac{z}{\lfloor n^{\beta}\rfloor}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{n^{\beta}}{\lfloor n^{\beta}\rfloor}\left(1+\frac{\eta_{\alpha}(-z)^{\alpha-1}}{\lfloor n^{\beta}\rfloor^{\alpha-1}}(1+o(1))\right)^{n}.

Since
\beta(\alpha-1)=1
, the right-hand side above converges as
n\to+\infty
for any
z\in(0,1)
to
\exp(\eta_{\alpha}(-z)^{\alpha-1})
. The pointwise convergence of the
S
-transforms implies the weak convergence of the underlying measures.
∎

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.9
(i), define

{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}^{\uparrow}=\frac{1}{n\,m_{1}(\mu)^{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Pi_{n,k}^{\uparrow},

where
\Pi_{n,k}^{\uparrow}={\mathbb{A}}_{1,k}^{1/2}\ldots{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1,k}^{1/2}{\mathbb{A}}_{n,k}{\mathbb{A}}_{n-1,k}^{1/2}\ldots{\mathbb{A}}_{1,k}^{1/2}
and
({\mathbb{A}}_{n,k})_{1\leq k\leq n}
is an array of freely identically distributed random variables with distribution
\mu
. Then,
({\mathbb{Y}}_{n}^{\uparrow})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a non-negative, non-commutative martingale with unit mean. By Corollary
3.8
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}({\mathbb{Y}}_{n}^{\uparrow})=\frac{1}{n^{2}m_{1}(\mu)^{2n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{Var}(\Pi_{n,k}^{\uparrow})=\frac{1}{n\,m_{1}(\mu)^{2n}}\mathrm{Var}(\mu^{\boxtimes n})=\frac{\mathrm{Var}(\mu)}{m_{1}(\mu)^{2}}.

Thus, we obtain
\sup_{n}\tau(({\mathbb{Y}}_{n}^{\uparrow})^{2})<+\infty
. Therefore, by
[
14
, Proposition 8]
,
({\mathbb{Y}}_{n}^{\uparrow})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges almost uniformly and in
L^{2}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}},\tau)
.

Remark 6.10
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}
and assume that
m_{p}(\mu)<+\infty
for some
p\in\mathbb{N}
. By the definition of
{\mathbb{Y}}_{n}
from Theorem
6.9
(i), we have

\kappa_{p}({\mathbb{Y}}_{n})=\frac{\kappa_{p}(\Pi_{n})}{n^{p-1}m_{1}(\mu)^{p\,n}}.

In (
3.2
), we proved that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\kappa_{\alpha}({\mathbb{Y}}_{n})=\frac{(\gamma\alpha)^{\alpha-1}}{\alpha!}=\kappa_{\alpha}({\mathbb{Y}}),\qquad\alpha\leq p,

where the last equality follows from
[
31
, Theorem 4.1 (3)]
.
This implies the convergence of moments of order less than or equal to
p
and uniform integrability of
({\mathbb{Y}}_{n}^{p})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
(see, e.g.,
[
9
, Theorem 3.6]
).
